Stochastic Calculus in Infinite Dimensions and SPDEs
Daniel Goodair

Abstract




Introduction

Stochastic Differential Equations have rich applications in physics and finance, for example in Langevin Equations modelling the movement of a particle in space [
5
] or the Black-Scholes options pricing model for the dynamics of the price of a stock [
3
]. These are applications of classical Itô Calculus, where we construct the integral of a process taking values in Euclidean space. Whilst this theory is adequate in such applications, mathematical models for physical phenomena far exceed those for the position of a particle. These notes ponder stochastic equations modelling a function of both space and time, such as the velocity or temperature of a particle. It is, therefore, necessary to define the stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}

(1)

for
\bm{\Psi}:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
W
a standard real valued Brownian Motion. We regard
\bm{\Psi}
not as a pointwise defined function but rather an element of a function space, which is our motivating context for stochastic integration of Hilbert Space valued processes. This is the sense in which we mean ‘Stochastic Calculus in Infinite Dimensions’, or at least partly, as the field of stochastic modelling has also expanded into infinite dimensional
driving processes
. Our notes assume only a rudimentary understanding of functional analysis and real valued stochastic calculus, with which we explore the following three main areas of this text. These areas are ordered in increasing complexity and novelty.

•

In Section
1
we present a ‘classical’ construction of the Itô stochastic integral, for processes evolving in a Hilbert Space. This occurs first for a one dimensional driving Brownian Motion, before generalisations to other one dimensional martingales and further to
Cylindrical Brownian Motion
. Our construction is direct and designed to be familiar to a reader who has undertaken the real valued study so well explicated in the likes of [
16
,
18
]. In defining our infinite dimensional Brownian Motion, that is the Cylindrical Brownian Motion, we cover the fundamentals of martingale theory in Hilbert Spaces broadly by finite dimensional projections along with the real valued theory. The hope is again that this approach is accessible to a reader with background in the real valued theory. The Cylindrical Brownian Motion is the only infinite dimensional driving process that we integrate with respect to; whilst we present a background on general
Q-
Cylindrical Processes which could be viable integrators, limiting ourselves to Cylindrical Brownian Motion enables the integral to be established as a straightforwards limit of the integrals against finite dimensional Brownian Motions. In particular we avoid the operator theoretic technicalities necessary in the general case, noting that our construction is sufficient for the framework and applications that follow.

•

Section
2
details a framework for the study of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs), which are evolution equations involving integration of the form introduced in the previous section. Through this framework we define notions of solutions for an abstract SPDE, motivated in particular by the recent attention given to transport type noise (where the stochastic integral is dependent on the gradient of the solution). This attention comes largely from the seminal work [
14
], in which Holm establishes a new class of stochastic equations driven by
Stratonovich
transport type noise which serve as fluid dynamics models by adding uncertainty in the transport of fluid parcels to reflect the unresolved scales. A rigorous mathematical understanding of these equations appears perilous for three key reasons. The first is the typical nonlinearity of fluid equations, rendering the more established linear theory insufficient. The second is the gradient dependency in the noise, taking us beyond the most general ‘variational frameworks’ seen in the literature as these are posed for a noise operator which is bounded on some Hilbert Space. The third is the Stratonovich integration, which we are likely to only understand as a corrected Itô integral, yet this conversion is highly nontrivial for a noise which is not bounded on a Hilbert Space.

More precisely, we present a framework which shares its spirit with the variational approach to SPDEs pioneered by Pardoux in the 1970s and now best represented in the more recent books [
20
,
21
]. This classical framework considers an evolution equation with respect to a Gelfand Triple, say
V\xhookrightarrow{}H\xhookrightarrow{}V^{*}
, where solutions have paths which are square integrable in
V
, continuous in
H
and satisfying an identity in
V^{*}
. Recalling our motivation of fluid equations, the prototypical example in this framework is the Navier-Stokes Equation. Whilst analytically weak solutions fit this framework seamlessly, analytically strong solutions fit to the spaces
W^{2,2}\xhookrightarrow{}W^{1,2}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}
which prompts our shift to a triplet of embedded Hilbert Spaces without any necessary duality structure. Furthermore to include a Stratonovich transport type noise we in fact introduce a fourth Hilbert Space, necessary as the Itô-Stratonovich correction costs a derivative for transport type noise. We can then properly define weak, strong, local and maximal solutions of nonlinear PDEs with Stratonovich transport noise, amongst other more familiar stochastic perturbations. We believe that presenting the technical details, in such generality, of these notions here facilitates the rigorous and free analysis of the equations in future works.

Some details of the proof of an energy equality in this setting are also presented. This is well understood in the typical variational framework, for which we again refer to [
21
], but we take care in addressing subtle changes. The first is the loss of the duality structure, though for this result we do assume a bilinear form relation which behaves similarly. The second is that we conduct the proof for local solutions, necessary for our motivating class of equations, so it is important for us to explicitly address how the localisation affects the proof. Similarly the final key change is that we do not assume any integrability over the probability space of our processes, demanding again another source of localisation which we find worthy of detailing in these notes. The section rounds out with a simple statement of the general Itô Formula, referring to [
6
] for a proof, and a demonstration that the infinite dimensional noise can be reduced to something one dimensional if it is constant multiplicative in each direction.

•

Section
3
concludes these notes and contains more advanced and novel techniques in the existence theory for nonlinear SPDEs. The beauty of the classical variational approach comes from the existence results, which certainly cannot be matched as elegantly in a framework built for 3D Navier-Stokes Equations and related stochastic fluid models. Instead we focus on techniques that can be used in this direction, centred around the
Galerkin Method
in which finite dimensional approximations of the SPDE are considered and some properties are used to deduce their limit. Immediately then an existence result for the finite dimensional equations is required, more precisely for where the Hilbert Space in which the equation evolves is finite dimensional but the driving Brownian Motion is still infinite dimensional. We assume standard Lipschitz and linear growth conditions, and to the best of our knowledge this result is not present in the literature. There are two predominant ways to deduce the existence of a limit of the finite dimensional approximations, which we detail now.

The first is through
tightness
, which is the stochastic route to relative compactness arguments used in PDE theory. The idea is that from tightness we can deduce relative compactness of the laws of the processes over some suitable function space, at which point Skorohod’s Representation Theorem enables the deduction of a limiting process almost surely on a new probability space. Criteria to deduce tightness in relevant function spaces are thus of great significance, and our criteria comes largely from the works of [
1
,
15
,
23
]. The second is through a Cauchy type argument in the relevant spaces, difficult to execute in the case of local solutions but recently this has been overcome to great effect due to Glatt-Holtz and Ziane in [
8
] and extended by the author [
10
]. We defer a greater discussion of this highly technical result to Section
3
.

In addition, we do give a complete set of assumptions necessary to deduce the existence of maximal solutions in this framework. The result is taken from the author’s work [
9
] so no proof is given here, but the methods discussed throughout these notes are used in the result. The purpose of including this is not for the reader to understand why it is true from this work, but rather to provide an idea of what kind of impositions one must make to deduce the existence of solutions to the highly non-trivial SPDEs motivating these notes. Of course a reference to the full proof is given for the interested reader. Our exposition wraps up with applications of the framework and results to the Navier-Stokes Equation under Stochastic Advection by Lie Transport, which is the aforementioned perturbation scheme developed in [
14
]. We hope that this conveys how the framework developed here is sufficient to properly understand such challenging SPDEs, and demonstrates concrete use of the techniques surveyed.

1
Stochastic Calculus in Infinite Dimensions

1.1
Elementary Notation

Throughout these notes we work with a fixed filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t}),\mathbbm{P})
, which is complete with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{0}
. We always consider Banach Spaces as measure spaces equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra, and shall use
\lambda
to represent the Lebesgue Measure. All of our Hilbert Spaces are assumed to be separable.

Definition 1.1.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mu)
denote a general measure space,
(\mathcal{Y},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}})
and
(\mathcal{Z},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{Z}})
be Banach Spaces, and
(\mathcal{U},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{U}})
,
(\mathcal{H},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
be general Hilbert spaces.

•

L^{p}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})
is the usual class of measurable
p-
integrable functions from
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{Y}
,
1\leq p<\infty
, which is a Banach space with norm

\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}^{p}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\lVert\phi(x)\rVert^{p}_{\mathcal{Y}}\mu(dx).

The space
L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})
is a Hilbert Space when
\mathcal{Y}
itself is Hilbert, with the standard inner product

\left\langle\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left\langle\phi(x),\psi(x)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\mu(dx).

•

L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})
is the usual class of measurable functions from
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{Y}
which are essentially bounded, which is a Banach Space when equipped with the norm

\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}:=\inf\{C\geq 0:\lVert\phi(x)\rVert_{Y}\leq C\textnormal{ for $\mu$-$a.e.$ }x\in\mathcal{X}\}.

•

C(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})
is the space of continuous functions from
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{Y}
.

•

C_{w}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})
is the space of ‘weakly continuous’ functions from
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{Y}
, by which we mean continuous with respect to the given topology on
\mathcal{X}
and the weak topology on
\mathcal{Y}
.

•

\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{Y};\mathcal{Z})
is the space of bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{Y}
to
\mathcal{Z}
. This is a Banach Space when equipped with the norm

\lVert F\rVert_{\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{Y};\mathcal{Z})}=\sup_{\lVert y\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}=1}\lVert Fy\rVert_{\mathcal{Z}}.

It is the dual space
\mathcal{Y}^{*}
when
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
, with operator norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}^{*}}.

•

\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};\mathcal{H})
is the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
\mathcal{U}
to
\mathcal{H}
, defined as the elements
F\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{U};\mathcal{H})
such that for some basis
(e_{i})
of
\mathcal{U}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert Fe_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}<\infty.

This is a Hilbert space with inner product

\left\langle F,G\right\rangle_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};\mathcal{H})}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle Fe_{i},Ge_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

which is independent of the choice of basis (see e.g. [
21
] Remark B.0.4).

•

For any
T>0
,
\mathscr{S}_{T}
is the subspace of
C\left([0,T];[0,T]\right)
of strictly increasing functions.

•

For any
T>0
,
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{Y}\right)
is the space of cádlág functions from
[0,T]
into
\mathcal{Y}
. It is a complete separable metric space when equipped with the metric

d(\phi,\psi):=\inf_{\eta\in\mathscr{S}_{T}}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\eta(t)-t\right|\vee\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\phi(t)-\psi(\eta(t))\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}}\right]

which induces the so called Skorohod Topology (see [
2
] pp124 for details).

•

The total variation of a function
\phi:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
,
V_{\mathcal{Y}}^{T}(\phi)
, is defined as

V_{\mathcal{Y}}^{T}(\phi):=\sup_{I}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\lVert\phi(t_{i+1})-\phi(t_{i})\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}}

for the supremum taken over all partitions
I=\left\{0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}=T\right\}
. A function
\psi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is said to be of bounded-variation if
V_{\mathcal{Y}}^{T}(\psi)<\infty
for all
T\geq 0
.

For the reader’s convenience we also collect notation that is introduced in the paper, referencing where it is defined as relevant.

•

\lambda
denotes the Lebesgue measure;

•

\mathbbm{1}_{A}
denotes the indicator function of the set
A
;

•

\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}
is defined in
1.2.3
;

•

\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
is defined in
1.2.4
;

•

\mathcal{M}^{2},\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
are defined in
1.3.1
;

•

\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
is defined in
1.5.2
;

•

\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c},\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
are the corresponding semi-martingale spaces;

•

\mathcal{I}^{T}_{M},\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
are defined in
1.3.2
;

•

\mathcal{I}^{T}_{\widetilde{M}},\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\widetilde{M}}
are defined in
1.3.3
;

•

[\cdot]
is defined in
1.5.7
;

•

[\cdot,\cdot]
is defined in
1.5.15
;

•

\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
is defined in
1.6.1
;

•

\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
is defined in
1.6.2
;

•

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
is defined in
1.6.5
;

•

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
is defined in
1.6.6
.

1.2
A Classical Construction for Hilbert Space Valued Processes

As alluded to, the construction precisely mirrors the standard one dimensional Itô integral; as such we start from simple processes.
\mathcal{H}
will denote a general Hilbert space, with norm and inner product
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
respectively.
W
represents a standard one dimensional Brownian Motion with respect to the fixed filtered probability space.

Definition 1.2.1
.

Let
0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{i}<t_{i+1}<\dots
be a time index such that
(t_{i})
approaches infinity. A simple
\mathcal{H}
valued process is one that for
a.e.\ \omega
takes the form

\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)=a_{0}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}(t)+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{(t_{i},t_{i+1}]}(t)

where each
a_{i}\in L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
and is
\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}}-
measurable, with respect to the Borel sigma algebra on
\mathcal{H}
. The limit is taken in
\mathcal{H}
.

Definition 1.2.2
.

The Itô integral of a simple
\mathcal{H}
valued process
\bm{\Psi}
, with respect to Brownian motion, is defined as

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}\big(W_{t_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t_{i}\wedge t}\big).

Note that in reality the above is a finite sum, so there is no danger in how we take the limit. Indeed it can alternatively be expressed as

\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}a_{i}\big(W_{t_{i+1}}-W_{t_{i}}\big)+a_{k}\big(W_{t}-W_{t_{k}}\big)

(2)

where
k
is such that
t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}].
Unsurprisingly now we define the integral for a more general class of integrands, using approximations by simple processes.

Definition 1.2.3
.

We use
\mathcal{I}_{T}^{\mathcal{H}}
to denote the class of
\mathcal{H}
valued processes
\bm{\Psi}
which are progressively measurable
1
1

1

Here and throughout, progressive measurability is defined with respect to the fixed filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
in the filtered probability space.
and satisfy the square integrability condition

\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)<\infty.

(3)

In other words,
\bm{\Psi}\in L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{H}\big)
where the domain space
\Omega\times[0,T]
is a measure space equipped with the product measure
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda
.
2
2

2

The progressive measurability of
\bm{\Psi}
ensures that it is measurable over this product space, and Tonelli’s Theorem justifies exchanging the order of integration.

We have made the definition for
progressively measurable
processes, not
previsible
processes as will commonly be seen in the literature. Progressive measurability is a weaker condition than previsibility, but thankfully most reasonably behaved processes (adapted and left continuous for example) are both progressively measurable and previsible. We make the definition here for the more general class of integrands
in the cases where the integrator is continuous
. Other authors may opt for previsible processes as these become necessary in retaining nice properties (e.g. martingality) when defining the stochastic integral with respect to discontinuous integrators, or even in making the definition itself.

Definition 1.2.4
.

The class of processes
\bm{\Psi}
such that
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}^{\mathcal{H}}
for all
T>0
is denoted by
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
.

\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
represents our class of integrands for all times, though there will be nothing wrong with defining the integral for times
t<T
in the class
\mathcal{I}_{T}^{\mathcal{H}}
. The construction comes as a limit of simple integrals, for which we need the following proposition which holds no differently to [
18
] Lemma 3.1.5 for example.

Proposition 1.2.5
.

For any
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}^{\mathcal{H}}
, there exists a sequence of simple processes
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
which converge to
\bm{\Psi}
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{H}\big)
.

Definition 1.2.6
.

We define the Itô stochastic integral for processes
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
by

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}^{n}dW_{s},

(4)

where
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is the sequence of simple processes postulated in Proposition
1.2.5
which approach
\bm{\Psi}
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathcal{H}\big),
and the limit is taken in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big).

The fact that this is the natural topology in which to take the limit of simple stochastic integrals falls from the Itô Isometry for simple processes, which further justifies that the construction is independent of the choice of simple approximation.

Proposition 1.2.7
.

For a simple process
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
and any time
t>0
,

\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\Big|\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s}\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Bigg)=\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}^{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big).

(5)

Proof.

Let’s suppose that
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
takes the form

\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega)=a^{n}_{0}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}(t)+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}^{n}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(t)

(6)

as outlined in Definition
1.2.1
. Then applying Definition
1.2.2
, we deconstruct the LHS of (
5
):

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\Big|\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s}\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\Big|\Big|\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{i}\big(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\big)\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\Big\langle\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{i}\big(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\big),\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{j}\big(W_{t^{n}_{j+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{j}\wedge t}\big)\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\big(\left\langle a^{n}_{i},a^{n}_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t})(W_{t^{n}_{j+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{j}\wedge t})\big)

recalling once more that the infinite sum is actually a finite sum (
2
) so there is no difficulty in extracting it from the inner product and expectation. For
i\neq j
, and without loss of generality
i<j
, the random inner product is
\mathcal{F}_{t_{j}}-
measurable as the continuity of the inner product preserves measurability, and therefore
\left\langle a^{n}_{i},a^{n}_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t})
and
(W_{t^{n}_{j+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{j}\wedge t})
are independent from the independent increments of Brownian Motion. The terms thus vanish and we are left with

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg(\lVert a^{n}_{i}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t})^{2}\bigg)

to which we note independence again and assert that this is just

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\big(\lVert a^{n}_{i}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)\big(t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t-t^{n}_{i}\wedge t\big)

which is precisely the integral

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\big(\lVert a^{n}_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)ds.

We can write

\left\|a^{n}_{i}\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)=\left\|a^{n}_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}},

the infinite sum of which is a single non-zero term, equal to

\left\|a^{n}_{0}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}(s)+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{i}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

at every
s
except for zero which is a set of Lebesgue measure zero in
[0,t]
. Again the infinite sum being only a single non-zero term justifies its exchange with expectation, and the above is of course
\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
, justifying the result.

∎

So, why is this useful in terms of the limit in (
4
)? First and foremost it ensures that the limit is uniquely defined; given that
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
is complete we need only show that the sequence of stochastic integrals is Cauchy in this space. The Itô Isometry tells us that
\big(\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s}\big)
is Cauchy in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
if and only if
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathcal{H}\big)
, which is of course true as by definition the
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
are convergent (to
\bm{\Psi}
) in this space. Furthermore the Isometry extends to the general integral defined in Definition
1.2.6
, as a trivial corollary of the discussion here.

Corollary 1.2.7.1
.

The Itô Isometry (
5
) holds for all processes
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}.

Without direct appeal to the formal construction, we may also understand the integral (
1
) as a random element of the dual space
\mathcal{H}^{*}
and identify the functional with its counterpart in
\mathcal{H}
in the usual sense.

Theorem 1.2.8
.

The Itô stochastic integral defined in Definition
1.2.6
is the unique element of
\mathcal{H}
satisfying the duality relation

\Big\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{s}

(7)

for all
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
. The above are random inner products, defined by

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}(\omega):=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega),\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

and similarly for the LHS. Therefore by the Riesz-Representation Theorem, it is consistent to define the Itô stochastic integral as an
\mathcal{H}^{*}
valued random variable via the mapping

\phi\mapsto\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{s}\bigg)(\omega).

Proof.

Given that we have defined (
1
) as a limit of simple processes, it will come as no surprise that we must use this approach to prove the relation (
7
). We will demonstrate that this holds for simple processes
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
, and later that it is preserved in the
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathcal{H}\big)
limit. Firstly though we ought to verify that the RHS of (
7
) makes sense, that is to say
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is a valid (one dimensional) integrand. Thus we must show the standard progressive measurability and square integrability conditions: for the former, note that the progressive measurability of
\bm{\Psi}
is preserved under composition with the continuous mapping
\left\langle\cdot,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.
The latter is straightforwards, as

\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)\leq\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)

(8)

which is finite by (
3
). Let’s suppose that
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
takes the form (
6
). Then applying Definition
1.2.2
, we deconstruct the LHS of (
7
):

\displaystyle\Big\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{i}\big(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\big),\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a^{n}_{i}\big(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\big),\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a^{n}_{i},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\big)

and proceed similarly for the RHS, observing that the integrand

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle a^{n}_{0}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}(s)\big)+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{i}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)\big),\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle a^{n}_{0}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}(s)\big),\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a^{n}_{i}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)\big),\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle a^{n}_{0},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}(s)\big)+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a^{n}_{i},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}(s)\big)

is again simple (this is completely analogous to showing that
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
was a valid integrand). Applying Definition
1.2.2
to the above proves the result for simple processes, so all that remains to show is preservation in the limit. We have of course

\displaystyle\Big\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

and a reminder that this limit is taken in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big).
We would like to take the limit outside of the inner product, in some appropriate topology, and use the result for simple functions: the steps would be

\displaystyle\Big\langle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Big\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{s}

so it should be clear that the topology we want to take this limit in is that of
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
, as the last line would be precisely the RHS of (
7
) by definition if we can show that the simple real valued process
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
converges to
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R}\big).
Thankfully it is straightforwards to justify taking this limit outside of the inner product: if
(f_{n})
converges to
f
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
then

\mathbbm{E}\Big(\left\langle f_{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle f,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big)^{2}=\mathbbm{E}\Big(\left\langle f_{n}-f,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big)^{2}\leq\mathbbm{E}\Big(\lVert f_{n}-f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big)=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\lVert f_{n}-f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big)\longrightarrow 0

so
(\left\langle f_{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
converges to
\left\langle f,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
, as required to justify the interchange. To show the convergence of
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
to
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R}\big)
we apply the same trick:

\displaystyle\lVert\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lVert\left\langle\bm{\Psi}-\bm{\Psi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R}\big)}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)

\displaystyle=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)

\displaystyle=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\lVert\bm{\Psi}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathcal{H}\big)}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0

where convergence to
0
is by definition of the approximating sequence
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
.

∎

We provide two applications of this result below, both of which will be fundamental to our SPDE framework.

Proposition 1.2.9
.

The Itô Isometry holds for a multi dimensional driving Brownian motion, in the sense that if
(\bm{\Psi}^{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are a collection of processes in
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
, and
(W^{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are independent Brownian Motions, then

\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\Big|\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Bigg)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}^{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\Big)

Proof.

We look to simplify the left hand side of the required equality, swiftly applying Parseval’s identity for a basis
(e_{k})
of
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\Big|\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s}dW^{i}_{s},e_{k}\Big\rangle^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{s}\Big)^{2}

having used linearity of the inner product to pull out the sum and Theorem
1.2.8
. We can now regard the infinite sum as an integral with respect to the counting measure and apply Tonelli’s Theorem, also expanding the square to obtain

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{s}\Big)^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{s}\Big)\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{j}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{s}\Big).

For the cross terms
i\neq j
we make use of the independence of the Brownian Motions and hence the respective stochastic integrals, as well as the standard property that the Itô integral has zero expectation to nullify these terms. Our expression reduces to

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{s}\Big)^{2}

to which we can apply the Itô Isometry (Corollary
1.2.7.1
for the Hilbert Space
\mathbb{R}
, which is of course the standard Isometry) giving us

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds

from which we apply Tonelli twice more to take the infinite sum all the way through,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{i}_{s},e_{k}\right\rangle^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds.

A final application of Parseval’s identity gives the result.

∎

Whilst we chose to prove Proposition
1.2.7
and subsequently Corollary
1.2.7.1
from first principles in the Hilbert Space setting, the method of proof here touches upon a fundamental aspect of this theory: with a good understanding of the standard
\mathbb{R}
valued setting, we can apply Theorem
1.2.8
to straightforwardly deduce key properties here. Indeed if we accepted the Itô Isometry in
\mathbb{R}
, we could have just proven Corollary
1.2.7.1
in the simple vein of Proposition
1.2.9
. We take this approach in extending the result of Theorem
1.2.8
.

Theorem 1.2.10
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
are Hilbert spaces such that
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
and
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1};\mathcal{H}_{2})
. Then the process
T\bm{\Psi}
defined by

T\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)=T\big(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\big)

belongs to
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
and is such that

T\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}\Big)=\int_{0}^{t}T\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}.

(9)

Proof.

We shall prove first that
T\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
. The progressive measurability is preserved under the continuity of
T
, and for
C
the (square of the) boundedness constant associated to
T
we have that at any time
t
,

\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert T\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}^{2}ds\Big)\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}^{2}ds\Big)<\infty

as
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
, showing that
T\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
. To commute
T
with the integral we shall use the characterisation from Theorem
1.2.8
, having now established that the right hand side of (
9
) is well defined in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. We introduce
T^{*}\in\mathscr{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{2};\mathcal{H}_{1}\right)
as the adjoint of
T
, and observe that for any
\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\Big\langle T\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}\Big),\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s},T^{*}\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},T^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}dW_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle T\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}dW_{s}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\int_{0}^{t}T\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s},\phi\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}

applying Theorem
1.2.8
twice. As this equality holds for arbitrary
\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
then we have proven (
9
), which is of course an identity
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

∎

1.3
Martingale and Local Martingale Integrators

As expected, we can extend the definition to integrators beyond Brownian motion, in the same manner as the standard Itô integral. We begin the extension to continuous square integrable martingales, and then to continuous local martingales.

Definition 1.3.1
.

We shall denote the class of real valued martingales
M
such that
M_{t}\in L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
for every
t\geq 0
by
\mathcal{M}^{2}
. The subclass of such martingales with
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
continuous paths will be represented by
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
.

Definition 1.3.2
.

For any
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
, define
\mathcal{I}_{M}^{T}
to be the class of
\mathcal{H}
valued processes
\bm{\Psi}
which are progressively measurable on
[0,T]\times\Omega
and satisfy the square integrability condition

\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[M]_{s}\Big)<\infty

where
\left[M\right]_{\cdot}
is the quadratic variation of
M_{\cdot}
. We similarly define
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
to be the class of processes
\bm{\Psi}
such that
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}_{M}^{T}
for all
T>0
.

We do not put the space
\mathcal{H}
explicitly into the time restricted notation for simplicity; once constructed, we will rarely need this notation, and when needed the space will be mentioned separately. Constructing the integral

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dM_{s}

for
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
now falls from what we have already done for (
1
). We use simple processes
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
as in Definition
1.2.1
to approximate
\bm{\Psi}
, in the sense that

\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}^{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[M]_{s}\Big)=0.

Simply replacing
W
by
M
in Definitions
1.2.2
and
1.2.6
completes the construction, though we do not give the details here. Let’s now move on to the more delicate matter of integration with respect to a local martingale. This begins again with notation for our set of integrands.

Definition 1.3.3
.

For a continuous local martingale
\widetilde{M}
, define
\mathcal{I}^{T}_{\widetilde{M}}
to be the class of progressively measurable processes
\bm{\Psi}
such that

\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[\widetilde{M}]_{s}<\infty\quad a.e.

(10)

Also define
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\widetilde{M}}
to be those
\bm{\Psi}
in
\mathcal{I}^{T}_{\widetilde{M}}
for every
T
.

Suppose that
\widetilde{M}
is localised by the stopping times
(T_{n}).
Without loss of generality this sequence of stopping times can be chosen such that the stopped processes
\widetilde{M}^{T_{n}}
defined by

\widetilde{M}^{T_{n}}_{t}:=\widetilde{M}_{t\wedge T_{n}}

are bounded; if
(T_{n}^{\prime})
are localising stopping times, then we can simply set

T_{n}=T_{n}^{\prime}\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\lvert\widetilde{M}_{t}\rvert\geq n\}

so that for each
n
,
\widetilde{M}^{T_{n}}
is a bounded continuous martingale and hence in
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{2}
. Note of course that the new stopping times
(T_{n})
are still non-decreasing and approach infinity
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
by the pathwise continuity of
\widetilde{M}
. Continuing in this theme, for a process
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\widetilde{M}}
let’s define some more non-decreasing random times
(R_{n})
by

R_{n}:=n\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[\widetilde{M}]_{s}\geq n\}

(11)

taking the convention that the infimum of the empty set is infinite. The
(R_{n})
are stopping times as they are simply first hitting times of the continuous and adapted random variable

\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[\widetilde{M}]_{s}.

Again these times tend to infinity
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
by condition (
10
). Now define
\tau_{n}
by

\tau_{n}=R_{n}\wedge T_{n}

and the truncated processes
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
as

\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}:=\bm{\Psi}_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}.

We use the fact that for
m\leq n
, and
t\leq\tau_{m}
, we have

\bm{\Psi}_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}=\bm{\Psi}_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{m}}

and also that

\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{n}}_{t}=\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{m}}_{t}

so we can make the consistent definition that

\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\widetilde{M}_{s}\Big)(\omega):=\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{n}}_{s}\Big)(\omega)

(12)

at almost every
\omega
for any
n
such that
t\leq\tau_{n}(\omega)
, noting that such an
n
exists (for almost every
\omega
). There is subtlety in this, as
n
itself is a random variable (it is dependent on
\omega
) and the logic in which we are proceeding is vital. To be clear, we are
not
considering the
n
of (
12
) as a random variable; the random selection of
n
occurs prior. That is to say we understand (
12
) as a definition of the left hand side pointwise
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
by fixing such an
\omega
, then fixing our
n
as outlined, and then considering the right hand side as a process which can be evaluated at any
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
, but is such that if
\omega^{\prime}\neq\omega
then it will not necessarily be true that
t\leq\tau_{n}(\omega^{\prime})
. We simply evaluate this process at
\omega
to make the definition. Of course to do this we require that at this choice of
n
,
\bm{\Psi}^{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{n}}}
: the process
(\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}})
is progressively measurable, as it is both left continuous and adapted (adaptedness becomes clear when for each fixed
t
, we write the random variable
\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}
as
1-\mathbbm{1}_{t>\tau_{n}}
). The square integrability in
1.3.2
comes from the fact that the random variable

\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{n}}]_{s}

is bounded by
n

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
(owing to (
11
)), hence the expectation satisfies the same bound. It is critical again here that the
n
in (
12
) is not allowed to be random, as we would have instead a bound

\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}d[\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{n}}]_{s}\bigg)(\omega)\leq n(\omega)

so we cannot deduce a finite expectation as required because the bound is not uniform in
\omega
. Of course where
\widetilde{M}
is itself a genuine martingale, this procedure defines the stochastic integral for processes with only the regularity (
10
). In this case we do not have to stop the integrator, just truncate the integrand.

Definition 1.3.4
.

In the special case where the continuous local martingale is given by the genuine martingale of Brownian Motion, we denote
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{W}
by simply
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}
. This class of processes differs to
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
because we only assume a bound almost everywhere, not in expectation.

We extend properties of the stochastic integral to this class of processes.

Proposition 1.3.5
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and
\phi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. Then
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and for every
t>0
we have that

\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

(13)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. The above are random inner products defined by

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}(\omega):=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega),\phi(\omega)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

and similarly for the left hand side.

Proof.

We should first justify that
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathbb{R}}
. The progressive measurability follows as for every
T>0
the mapping

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:t\times\omega\times\tilde{\omega}\rightarrow\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi(\tilde{\omega})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

is
\mathcal{B}([0,T])\times\mathcal{F}_{T}\times\mathcal{F}_{0}
measurable, so in particular it is
\mathcal{B}([0,T])\times\mathcal{F}_{T}\times\mathcal{F}_{T}
measurable and as such

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:t\times\omega\rightarrow\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi(\omega)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

is
\mathcal{B}([0,T])\times\mathcal{F}_{T}
measurable as required. Note that we have used the progressive measurability requirement on
\bm{\Psi}
. We also appreciate that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,

\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r}(\omega),\phi(\omega)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\leq\lVert\phi(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr<\infty

again by assumption on
\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
. Thus
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathbb{R}}
. To compute the integrals we introduce the stopping times

\tau_{j}:=j\wedge\inf\left\{0\leq t<\infty:(1+\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\geq j\right\}

such that for every
j\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
,
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{R}}
. For
\omega
fixed and
t
fixed as in (
13
) then we choose
n
sufficiently large so that
\tau_{j}(\omega)\geq t
. The integrals are then defined at this
\omega
by

\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau^{j}}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau^{j}},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

(14)

so we in fact show that (
14
) holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
for every
j
. We now fix arbitrary
j\in\mathbbm{N}
. Our plan is as follows: we consider a sequence of simple processes
(\bm{\Phi}^{n})
which approximate
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathcal{H})
as postulated in Proposition
1.2.5
. We then claim that
(\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
is a sequence of
\mathbb{R}
valued simple processes which converge to
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R})
. Following this we shall prove (
13
) for this simple case and show the identity holds in the limit.

We first show that for each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is a simple process. Let
\bm{\Phi}^{n}
have representation as in Definition
1.2.1
. Then

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle a^{n}_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle a^{n}_{0},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\}}+\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a_{i}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\mathbbm{1}_{(t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1}]}

so this would satisfy the requirements of an
\mathbb{R}
valued simple process if for each
i\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\left\langle a^{n}_{i},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
and is
\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}}-
measurable. For the square integrability constraint, observe that

\mathbbm{E}\left(\left\langle a^{n}_{i},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert a^{n}_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})}^{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert a^{n}_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)<\infty

by the assumptions of
a_{i}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
and
\phi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
. The
\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}}
measurability follows in the same way as the progressive measurability of
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Indeed the required
L^{2}\left(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R}\right)
convergence follows similarly as

\displaystyle\left\|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n}_{\cdot},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\lVert\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}-\bm{\Phi}^{n},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}-\bm{\Phi}^{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})}\left\|\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}-\bm{\Phi}^{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R})}

and by assumption

\left\|\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}-\bm{\Phi}^{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, so the convergence is proved. To show the identity (
13
) in the case of the simple process
\bm{\Phi}^{n}
, observe that

\displaystyle\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Phi}^{n}_{r}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{n}_{i}\big(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\big),\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a^{n}_{i}\left(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\right),\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\langle a^{n}_{i},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left(W_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t}-W_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

as required. In order to conclude the argument, by definition of the integral we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau^{j}}dW_{r}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Phi}^{n}_{r}dW_{r}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau^{j}},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

where the first limit is taken in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
and the second one in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
. For each we can thus extract a
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergent subsequence in the appropriate space, so by taking successive subsequences we can find one common subsequence indexed by
(n_{k})
such that the above limits hold
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. Thus

\displaystyle\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau^{j}}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Phi}^{n_{k}}_{r}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Phi}^{n_{k}}_{r}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Phi}^{n}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau^{j}},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

so (
14
) is justified and the proof is complete.

∎

We note that the
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurability requirement on
\phi
really comes into play in showing the
\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}}-
measurability of
\left\langle a^{n}_{i},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. If we were to consider the integral over some
[s,t]
interval instead, then one could relax
\phi
to only being
\mathcal{F}_{s}-
measurable. In fact the result can also be extended to unbounded
\phi
. To do this we shall prove a Stochastic Dominated Convergence Theorem.

Lemma 1.3.6
.

Let
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
be a sequence in
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
such that there exists processes
\bm{\Psi}:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
\bm{\Phi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
with the properties that for every
T>0
,
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.e.

(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T]
:

1.

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}
;

2.

(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega))
is convergent to
\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)
in
\mathcal{H}
.

Then
\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and for every
t>0
, there exists a subsequence indexed by
(n_{k})
such that

\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k}}_{r}dW_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}

(15)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
.

Proof.

Immediately we note that
\bm{\Psi}
inherits the progressive measurablity from
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
from the almost everywhere limit in the product space
\Omega\times[0,T]
when equipped with product sigma algebra
\mathcal{F}_{T}\times\mathcal{B}([0,T])
. Similarly we must have that for
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.e.

(\omega,t)
,
\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}
so
\bm{\Psi}
must too satisfy the integrability constraints and hence belongs to
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
. We look to find a common sequence of localising times for the stochastic integrals, and then demonstrate (
15
) by the showing the identity holds true when stopped at each localising time. To this end we introduce the stopping times

\tau_{j}:=j\wedge\inf\left\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\geq j\right\}

which from item
1
serve as a sequence of localising times for every
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
, and too for
\bm{\Psi}
. Thus for any fixed
t>0
and
j\in\mathbbm{N}
we wish to show that

\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k}}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}dW_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}dW_{r}

(16)

for a subsequence
(n_{k})

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
, or equivalently that

\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}(\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k}}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r})\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}dW_{r}=0.

We first assess the convergence in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
, applying Corollary
1.2.7.1
for each fixed
n
to see that

\mathbbm{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r})\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r})\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right).

Observing that for
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.e.

(\omega,t)
,

\displaystyle\lVert(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{r}(\omega))\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}(\omega)}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{r}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

Then with dominating function
4\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{j}}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
we can apply the standard Dominated Convergence Theorem for the integral over the product space (we face no problems with the order and configuration of integration from Tonelli’s Theorem given the progressive measurability) to deduce that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r})\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right)=0

and therefore

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r})\mathbbm{1}_{r\leq\tau_{j}}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=0.

Thus we have demonstrated the convergence (
16
) but for the whole sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
, from which we can deduce a
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergent subsequence and the result is proved.

∎

Proposition 1.3.7
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. Then
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and for every
t>0
we have that

\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

(17)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
.

Proof.

A justification that
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathbb{R}}
is precisely as in Proposition
1.3.5
. To apply this result, we rewrite
\phi
in a trivial way as

\phi:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\phi\mathbbm{1}_{k\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}<k+1}

where the limit is taken
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathcal{H}
(similarly to (
2
) this is just a finite sum at each fixed
\omega
, or more precisely just a single element of the sum). Introducing the notation

\phi^{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\phi\mathbbm{1}_{k\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}<k+1}

then clearly
\phi^{n}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
and is still
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable, so we can apply Proposition
1.3.5
to see that

\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r},\phi^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},\phi^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}.

We can take the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
limit in
\mathcal{H}
outside of the inner product on the left hand side, so it is sufficient to show that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},\phi^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}

(18)

or at least that this is true for a subsequence. This is an immediate application of Lemma
1.3.6
, with dominating function simply the limit
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.

∎

The same is true for multiplication by real valued random variables, where the proof is identical. We state the result here.

Proposition 1.3.8
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and
\eta:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. Then
\eta\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and for every
t>0
we have that

\eta\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}\eta\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
.

1.4
Cylindrical Processes

Having now addressed the question of how to integrate a Hilbert Space valued process with respect to a finite dimensional driving martingale, we look to extend this theory to the case of an infinite dimensional driving martingale. The aforementioned construction then arises from the one dimensional projections of the driving process. Our notion of infinite dimensional Brownian Motion is a Cylindrical Brownian Motion, which we explore in this subsection. Additional operator theory is used in this subsection to better understand the background of the process, however it is not necessary in the resulting integral constructed in Subsection
1.6
. We will denote by
Y
a real-valued zero-mean Gaussian process with correlation function
R(t,s)
,
\mathcal{H}
a Hilbert space and
Q
a bounded positive self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}.

Definition 1.4.1
.

A
Q
-Cylindrical
Y
process over
\mathcal{H}
is a process
X^{Q}
taking values in the space of functions from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{R}
, that is

X^{Q}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}

such that for each
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
X^{Q}_{h}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a process of zero mean Gaussian random variables, and for every
g,h\in\mathcal{H}
and all times
t,s
,

\mathbbm{E}\big(X^{Q}_{g}(t)X^{Q}_{h}(s)\big)=\left\langle Qg,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,s).

Definition 1.4.2
.

A
Q
-Cylindrical Brownian Motion is defined as above where the
Y
process is a Brownian Motion. A Cylindrical Brownian Motion is a
Q
-Cylindrical Brownian Motion such that
Q
is the identity operator.

To make this abstract definition workable, we look to how such processes can be represented. This motivates the notion of a regular process.

Definition 1.4.3
.

A
Q
-Cylindrical
Y
process
X^{Q}
is said to be regular if there exists a square integrable
\mathcal{H}
valued process
\widetilde{X}^{Q}
(that is,
\widetilde{X}^{Q}_{t}\in L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
for all
t\geq 0
) such that for every
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
X^{Q}_{h}
has the same law as the process
\left\langle\widetilde{X}^{Q},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
defined by

\left\langle\widetilde{X}^{Q},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}(t,\omega):=\left\langle\widetilde{X}^{Q}(t,\omega),h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

We will not hesitate to identify the functional valued process
X^{Q}
with the Hilbert space one
\widetilde{X}^{Q}.
In the next theorem however, we keep the distinction for clarity.

Theorem 1.4.4
.

A
Q
-Cylindrical
Y
process
X^{Q}
is regular if and only if
Q
is trace-class. In this case
X^{Q}
admits the regular representation

\widetilde{X}^{Q}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t}

(19)

where the
(e_{i})
are an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
consisting of eigenvectors of the self-adjoint trace-class (hence compact) operator
Q
,
(\lambda_{i})
are the corresponding eigenvalues, and
(Y^{i})
are independent copies of the process
Y
. The limit is taken in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big).

Proof.

We consider the two directions. In the first one we show that if
Q
is trace-class then (
19
) is a regular representation of
X^{Q}
. In the second we show that if
X^{Q}
is regular then
Q
is trace-class, so by the first implication, (
19
) is again a regular representation of
X^{Q}
.

\impliedby
:

A sensible place to start would be to verify that for trace-class
Q
, (
19
) does indeed define an element of
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big).
We will rely on completeness of the space and show that the sequence of partial sums is Cauchy. Observe that

\mathbbm{E}\big(\lVert\sum_{i=m}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\big)=\mathbbm{E}\big(\sum_{i=m}^{n}\lvert\lambda_{i}\rvert\lvert Y^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\big)=\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert Y_{t}\rvert^{2}\big)\sum_{i=m}^{n}\lambda_{i}.

All we then require to conclude the Cauchy property is that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}<\infty
which it is given that
Q
is trace class. To conclude that (
19
) is a regular representation of
X^{Q}
, it is sufficient to show that the one dimensional processes
\left\langle\widetilde{X}^{Q},h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy the conditions postulated of the
X^{Q}_{h}
in Definition
1.4.1
as these characterise the distribution.

First of all for each
h\in\mathcal{H}
and time
t
, we must verify that the random variable

\omega\mapsto\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t}(\omega),h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(20)

is zero mean Gaussian. Note that

\Big\langle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

where the second limit is in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
just as we did in Theorem
1.2.8
. The random variable (
20
) is thus an
L^{2}
limit of zero mean Gaussian random variables, so is itself zero mean Gaussian (convergence in
L^{2}
implies that in distribution so we have Gaussianity, and
L^{2}
convergence implies
L^{1}
from which we readily deduce the zero mean property). It remains to show that for each
g,h\in\mathcal{H}
and
s,t>0
that

\mathbbm{E}\bigg(\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t},g\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big\langle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}e_{i}Y^{j}_{s},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigg)=\left\langle Qg,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,s).

We take the limit through the first inner product on the LHS as above, that is

\mathbbm{E}\Bigg(\bigg(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t},g\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigg)\Big\langle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}e_{i}Y^{j}_{s},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Bigg)

(21)

and argue that for a sequence of functions
(f_{n})
convergent to
f
in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
, and
a\in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
, that

(\lim_{L^{2}}f_{n})(a)=\lim_{L^{1}}(f_{n}a).

The right side is well defined as the limit of a Cauchy sequence:

\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert f_{n}a-f_{m}a\rvert\big)=\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert(f_{n}-f_{m})a\rvert\big)\leq\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert f_{n}-f_{m}\rvert^{2}\big)\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert a\rvert^{2}\big)

and a similar calculation shows that this element of
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
is the left side. Applying to (
21
) and pulling the
L^{1}
limit through the expectation produces

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg(\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t},g\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big\langle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}e_{j}Y^{j}_{s},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigg)

and playing the same game, this is

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg(\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i}Y^{i}_{t},g\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big\langle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}e_{j}Y^{j}_{s},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigg)

and further

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big\langle\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i},g\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big\langle\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}e_{j},h\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y^{i}_{t}Y^{j}_{s}\bigg).

Independence of the copies
(Y^{i})
and definition of the correlation function gives that this is equal to

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\langle\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i},g\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big\langle\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}e_{i},h\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,s)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\langle\lambda_{i}e_{i},g\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big\langle e_{i},h\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,s)

which is just

\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}\left\langle e_{i},g\right\rangle e_{i},h\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,s)=\left\langle Qg,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,s)

as required.

\implies
:

For the reverse direction, assume that
X^{Q}
is regular with corresponding process
\widetilde{X}^{Q}
(which is not necessarily of the form (
19
)). We want an expression in terms of the trace of
Q
, so we exploit Definition
1.4.1
:

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert X^{Q}_{e_{i}}(t)\rvert^{2}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle Qe_{i},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}R(t,t)

and use an alternative expression from the assumed regular representation:

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\big(\lvert X^{Q}_{e_{i}}(t)\rvert^{2}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\big(\left\langle\widetilde{X}^{Q}(t),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big)=\mathbbm{E}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\widetilde{X}^{Q}(t),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big)=\mathbbm{E}\big(\lVert\widetilde{X}^{Q}(t)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big)<\infty

where the infinite sum is pulled inside the expectation from the monotone convergence theorem, and finiteness is by assumption on
\widetilde{X}^{Q}\in L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big).
Hence
Q
is trace-class, and by the first implication, (
19
) is a regular representation of
X^{Q}
.

∎

A standard Cylindrical Brownian Motion, that is where
Q
is the identity, is thus not regular. However it would be convenient to have such a representation for Cylindrical Brownian Motion (denote this
\mathcal{W}_{t}
); we would like this to be something along the lines of

\mathcal{W}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}e_{i}W^{i}_{t}

where the
(e_{i})
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
, and
(W^{i})
are standard independent Brownian Motions. We can in fact explicitly construct a larger Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
such that the inclusion mapping
J:\mathcal{H}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is Hilbert-Schmidt. The composition
Q:=JJ^{*}
is then trace-class on
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, and indeed
\mathcal{W}_{t}
is a
Q
-Cylindrical Brownian Motion on
\mathcal{H}
’: we defer the details to e.g. [
17
] Problem 3.2.6. To be precise, that is
J(e_{i})=\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\eta_{i}
for each
i
, where the
(\eta_{i})
form an orthonormal basis (of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
) of eigenfunctions of
JJ^{*}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
, and for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\mathcal{W}_{h}(t)=\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}J(e_{i})W^{i}_{t},J(h)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}.

1.5
Cylindrical and Hilbert Space Valued Martingales

As we look to make cylindrical processes the driving force in our stochastic integral, it will come as no surprise that we introduce martingality in this setting.

Definition 1.5.1
.

A
Q
-Cylindrical
Y
process over
\mathcal{H}
,
X^{Q}
, is said to be a martingale if for every
h\in\mathcal{H}
, the real valued process
X^{Q}_{h}
is a martingale.

Whilst the choice is natural, there seems no way to extend the definition to include two key aspects of our martingale integrators for a one dimensional driving process, namely continuity and square integrability. As such we consider the analogy in Hilbert spaces, covering at least the cylindrical processes which have a regular representation, with an understanding that this would most likely be the starting point for developing the integration theory with respect to an infinite dimensional martingale.

Definition 1.5.2
.

A process
M
taking values in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is said to be a martingale if for every
h\in\mathcal{H}
, the process
\left\langle M,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is a real valued martingale. The martingale is said to be continuous if for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
and every
T>0
,
M(\omega):[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is continuous. The martingale is said to be square integrable if for every
t\geq 0
,
\mathbbm{E}\big(\lVert M_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big)<\infty
. The class of continuous square integrable martingales will be denoted
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H}).
3
3

3

Recall that
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}:=\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, see Definition
1.3.1
.

We look to show that
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
is closed in a relevant topology, for which we shall use a sufficient condition for conitnuity proven now.

Lemma 1.5.3
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}\in C_{w}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
and
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\in C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
. Then
\bm{\Psi}\in C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
.

Proof.

We fix some
t\in[0,T]
and look to show that
\bm{\Psi}
is continuous at
t
. To this end consider arbitrary
s\in[0,T]
. Then

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right).

For any given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that for all
s\in[0\vee(t-\delta),(t+\delta)\wedge T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle<\frac{\varepsilon}{3},

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}

from each of the two assumptions, which gives the result.
∎

Proposition 1.5.4
.

Let
(M^{n})
be a sequence in
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
M
a process with values in
\mathcal{H}
such that at every time
t\geq 0
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert M^{n}_{t}-M_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)=0.

Then
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

The square integrability is trivial, so we just consider the martingale property and continuity. For every
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
, at every
t\geq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\left\langle M^{n}_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle M_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|\right)=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\left\langle M^{n}_{t}-M_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert M^{n}_{t}-M_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert M^{n}_{t}-M_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

which converges to zero as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In particular,
(\left\langle M^{n}_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
converges to
\left\langle M_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\right)
, and as the former is a sequence of real-valued martingales by definition, then it is standard that
\left\langle M,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is again a martingale. As
\phi
was arbitrary, we deduce the martingality of
M
. It remains to show pathwise continuity. For this we introduce the finite dimensional projections
(\mathcal{P}_{k})
, defined by

\mathcal{P}_{k}:\phi\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\phi,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}e_{i}

for
(e_{i})
a fixed orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
. The goal is to first show that
\mathcal{P}_{k}M
has continuous paths for each
k
; as
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is an orthogonal projection in
\mathcal{H}
, it is clear that at every
t\geq 0
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}\left(M^{n}_{t}-M_{t}\right)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)=0

and in particular

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M^{n}_{t}-M_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)=0.

We look to deduce some convergence for a real valued martingale and use the developed theory in this area, for which we note that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M^{n}_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|^{2}\right)\leq k\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M^{n}_{t}-M_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)=0

so the sequence in
n
,
\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M^{n},e_{i}\right\rangle\right)
, of real valued martingales is convergent in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\right)
at every
t\geq 0
. It is known, for example [
16
] Proposition 1.5.23, that
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
is closed in this topology hence
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle
is continuous. As this is true for every
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, we may take differences to see that
\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle
is continuous for every
i
, and therefore
\mathcal{P}_{k}M
is continuous in
\mathcal{H}
for each
k
(of course, still pathwise
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
). In fact we take the same approach as the referenced proposition, working directly with the sequence of submartingales
(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})
indexed by
k
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, by Doob’s Inequality ([
16
] Theorem 1.3.8), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\left\langle M_{t}(\omega),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}>\varepsilon\right\}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\left\langle M_{T},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

(22)

but we have already established that
\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert M_{T}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)<\infty
, or equivalently
\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle M_{t}(\omega),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)<\infty
. Therefore in the limit as
k\rightarrow\infty
, (
22
) approaches zero uniformly in
j>k
. One may choose a subsequence such that for all
k_{l}<k_{m}
,

\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=k_{l}+1}^{k_{m}}\left\langle M_{t}(\omega),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}>\frac{1}{l}\right\}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2^{l}}

so by the Borel Cantelli Lemma the subsequence
\sum_{i=1}^{k_{l}}\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
is Cauchy in
C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. It thus admits a limit
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
, which agrees with the limit at each
t
, given by
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle M_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
which is of course
\lVert M_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
. Therefore, the process
\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
is pathwise continuous; from Lemma
1.5.3
, it is now sufficient to just show weak continuity. This is clear however, as for any given
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
and
t\geq 0
, we have again that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left\langle M^{n}_{t}-M_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)=0

so
\left\langle M,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is shown to belong to
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
exactly as was done for
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, thus concluding the proof.

∎

Proposition 1.5.5
.

For
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
, the Itô stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{\cdot}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}

belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

Recalling Definition
1.2.6
, the integral is defined at each time
t
as a limit in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
of simple integrals. From Proposition
1.5.4
it is sufficient to show that these approximating integrals all belong to
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
, which is clear referring to the representation (
2
) and the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
.

∎

This result extends to the case of a general martingale integrator completely synonymous with the finite dimensional setting. Local martingality is then defined as we would expect, and we have the following result.

Proposition 1.5.6
.

For a continuous local martingale
\widetilde{M}
and
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\widetilde{M}}
, the Itô stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\widetilde{M}_{s}

is itself a continuous local martingale.

Proof.

We claim that the localising stopping times are given simply by the
(\tau_{n})
used in the definition of the integral. We have already seen that these tend to infinity almost surely, so it just remains to show that at any fixed
n\in\mathbbm{N}
the stopped process is a continuous martingale. We fix such
n
and look at how the stopped process

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\widetilde{M}_{s}

is actually defined. Well, at each fixed
\omega
we need to choose an
m
such that
t\wedge\tau_{n}(\omega)\leq\tau_{m}(\omega)
, so we can simply choose
m
to be
n
. Indeed this can be done uniformly across all
\omega
, so the stopped process is genuinely the integral

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\widetilde{M}^{\tau_{n}}_{s}

for this fixed
n
, which is a continuous martingale simply as in Proposition
1.5.5
.
∎

The next ingredient would be a definition of quadratic variation, which we look to do via a Doob-Meyer decomposition for
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
. That is, can we show that
\lVert M\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
defines a sub-martingale? Well we have integrability by definition, and

\lVert M_{t}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle M_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

where the limit is defined
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
. Again by definition the above projections are martingales and so the squares are sub-martingales. The process
\lVert M\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
is adapted as each
\lVert M_{t}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the
a.e.
limit of
\mathcal{F}_{t}-
measurable random variables (on the complete measure space), and it is a sub-martingale as we can apply the Monotone Convergence Theorem to take the limit through the expectation for the defining sub-martingale property. As such, we have the following.

Definition 1.5.7
.

For
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
, the quadratic variation
[M]
of
M
is defined to be the unique
4
4

4
