Uniqueness here is ‘up to indistinguishability’, which is to say there exists a set
A\in\mathcal{F}
with
\mathbbm{P}(A)=1
such that at all
t\geq 0
and all
\omega\in A
, the processes are equal.
continuous, adapted, non-decreasing process with
[M]_{0}=0
(
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
) specified in the Doob-Meyer decomposition such that

\lVert M\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-[M]

is a real valued martingale.

Proposition 1.5.8
.

Suppose that
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
, then

\left[\int_{0}^{\cdot}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}\right]_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr.

(23)

Proof.

The fact that the process (
23
) is continuous, adapted, non-decreasing and starting from zero is clear. It simply remains to show the required martingality. To this end observe that at each time
t
,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right)

having applied Theorem
1.2.8
to the first term and the Monotone Convergence Theorem to the second term, where the infinite sum is a limit taken
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. From the standard one dimensional theory, for each
n
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right)

is a real valued martingale so to conclude the proof we only need to justify that the
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
limit also holds in
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
as convergence in this space preserves martingality. This is a straightforwards application of the Monotone Convergence Theorem applied to each integral separately, which concludes the proof.

∎

We also look to reconcile this definition with the one often stated in the real valued case, as a limit in probability over any time partition with mesh approaching zero.

Proposition 1.5.9
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}
and consider any sequence of partitions

I_{l}:=\left\{0=t^{l}_{0}<t^{l}_{1}<\dots<t^{l}_{k_{l}}=T\right\}

with
\max_{j}|t^{l}_{j}-t^{l}_{j-1}|\rightarrow 0
as
l\rightarrow\infty
. Then for all
t\in[0,T]
, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

Proof.

We once again look to prove this by considering the finite dimensional projections on which the result is known to be true, before showing that it is preserved in the limit. As in the real valued case, [
16
] Theorem 1.5.8, we introduce a sequence of stopping times
(\tau^{n})
defined at every
n\in\mathbbm{N}
by

\tau^{n}:=n\wedge\inf\left\{t\in[0,T]:\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{r}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\geq n\right\}.

For every
n
we define the process

\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{\cdot}:=\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau^{n}}

and now look to show that

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\right|\right)=0.

(24)

As
(\tau^{n})
approach infinity
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
then (
24
) is sufficient to deduce the result, as in [
16
] Theorem 1.5.8. Identically to the proof of Proposition
1.5.8
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}dW_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right|\right)

where on the last line we have applied the Monotone Convergence Theorem. From [
16
] Theorem 1.5.8 we know that for each
i\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left[\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right]\right|\right)=0

so we would be done if we can justify the interchange of infinite sum and limit in
l
. We look to apply the Dominated Convergence Theorem (for the infinite sum again considered as an integral with counting measure), noting that for each fixed
i,l\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left[\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right]\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\mathbbm{E}\left[\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW_{r}\right)^{2}\right]+\mathbbm{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\mathbbm{E}\left[\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right]+\mathbbm{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=2\mathbbm{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r},e_{i}\right\rangle_{H}^{2}dr\right]

(25)

which is a bound uniform in
l
and summable in
i
. This is our dominating function which justifies the application of the Dominated Convergence Theorem, concluding the proof.

∎

Lemma 1.5.10
.

Suppose that
(M^{n})
is a sequence of martingales in
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
which at every time
t\geq 0
, converges in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
to some
M_{t}
. Suppose in addition that at any time
t\geq 0
, the sequence
\left([M^{n}]_{t}\right)
converges to some
\psi_{t}
in
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
where
\psi
is continuous, adapted and non-decreasing (
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
). Then
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
[M]
is indistinguishable from
\psi
.

Proof.

The fact that
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
is immediate from Proposition
1.5.4
, so we move on to the quadratic variation. Observe that for each
n
, by definition

\lVert M^{n}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-[M^{n}]

is a real valued martingale, so the
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
limit at each time
t
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big(\lVert M^{n}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-[M^{n}]\big)

is again a real valued martingale if it exists. But this is clear as it is just

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert M^{n}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[M^{n}]=\lVert M\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-\psi

by definition of the limit. From Definition
1.5.7
then if
\psi_{0}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
we have that
\psi
satisfies the conditions of the quadratic variation of
M
, so is indistinguishable from it. This property is immediate as
\psi_{0}
is the limit in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\right)
of
([M^{n}]_{0})
by assumption, whilst this is just a sequence of zeros
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
, hence the result.
∎

In part due to this result, we provide an alternative characterisation of the quadratic variation under some additional boundedness assumptions. We reintroduce the projections
(\mathcal{P}_{k})
used in Proposition
1.5.4
.

Proposition 1.5.11
.

For any
M\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
,
[M]
has the representation

[M]=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for the limit taken in
C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
for any
T\geq 0
.

Proof.

Observe that

\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

which is the sum of
k
one dimensional submartingales, and in particular

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\left[\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]\right)

is a martingale. From the definition of the quadratic variation it is thus clear that

\left[\mathcal{P}_{k}M\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left[\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right].

(26)

Moreover we have that at each
t\geq 0
,
\mathcal{P}_{k}M_{t}
is convergent to
M_{t}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathcal{H}
, and furthermore in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
from an application of the Monotone Convergence Theorem to the difference process
\lVert M-\mathcal{P}_{k}M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i=k+1}^{\infty}\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
. Reminiscent of Lemma
1.5.10
we look to show convergence of the sequence
\left(\left[\mathcal{P}_{k}M\right]\right)
to some
\psi
at each
t\geq 0
in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\right)
, and in fact we show the stronger convergence in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)\right)
for each
T\geq 0
. We proceed by showing the Cauchy property in this Banach Space. To do this we consider, for
j<k
,

[\mathcal{P}_{k}M]-[\mathcal{P}_{j}M]=\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\left[\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right].

(27)

From this identity and the preceding work, it is clear that

[\mathcal{P}_{k}M]-[\mathcal{P}_{j}M]=[\mathcal{P}_{k}M-\mathcal{P}_{j}M]

(28)

and therefore

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|[\mathcal{P}_{k}M]_{t}-[\mathcal{P}_{j}M]_{t}\right|=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}[\mathcal{P}_{k}M-\mathcal{P}_{j}M]_{t}=[\mathcal{P}_{k}M-\mathcal{P}_{j}M]_{T}.

(29)

Furthermore we are concerned with a control in expectation of this term. From the property that real valued martingales have constant expectation,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}M_{T}-\mathcal{P}_{j}M_{T}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-[\mathcal{P}_{k}M-\mathcal{P}_{j}M]_{T}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}M_{0}-\mathcal{P}_{j}M_{0}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-[\mathcal{P}_{k}M-\mathcal{P}_{j}M]_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}M_{0}-\mathcal{P}_{j}M_{0}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

so in particular

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left([\mathcal{P}_{k}M-\mathcal{P}_{j}M]_{T}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}M_{T}-\mathcal{P}_{j}M_{T}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-\lVert\mathcal{P}_{k}M_{0}-\mathcal{P}_{j}M_{0}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\left(\left\langle M_{T},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\left\langle M_{0},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\left\langle M_{T},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)+\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\left\langle M_{0},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right).

By the square integrability assumption on
M
we have that at every
t\geq 0
,
\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle M_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)<\infty
which justifies that the right hand side of the above approaches zero as
j\rightarrow\infty
, uniformly in
k
. With (
29
) we deduce that the sequence
([\mathcal{P}_{k}M])
is Cauchy in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)\right)
for each
T\geq 0
so admits a limit in this space which we call
\psi
. Through the
L^{1}\left(\Omega;C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)\right)
convergence we can deduce the existence of a subsequence which is
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergent in
C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
. In fact we can upgrade this to convergence over the whole sequence, as was note that
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
at each
t\geq 0
the real valued sequence
([\mathcal{P}_{k}M]_{t})
not only has a convergent subsequence but is non-decreasing in
k
, which implies convergence of the whole sequence. Convergence of the whole sequence
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
can then be deduced by the Cauchy property with (
29
) and (
28
). In summary thus far we have that

\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[\left\langle M,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right]

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for the limit taken in
C\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
for any
T\geq 0
. It only remains to show that
\psi
is indistinguishable from
[M]
, which we do by verifying the conditions of Lemma
1.5.10
. The convergence has already been established hence we need only the regularity on
\psi
. It is continuous by construction, and must be adapted as
\psi_{t}
is the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
limit of the
\mathcal{F}_{t}-
measurable
[\mathcal{P}_{k}M]_{t}
on the complete measure space. This limit similarly preserves the non-decreasing property, so
\psi
satisfies the required conditions and must be indistinguishable from
[M]
due to Lemma
1.5.10
.

∎

Following on from the quadratic variation we look to introduce the cross-variation between martingales. Guided by a motivation to use the Stratonovich integral, introduced in Subsection
2.1
, we will need to consider the cross-variation between elements of
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
. Defining this akin to a polarisation identity is out of the question as one cannot take sums of these martingales, so we look to use the characterisation in terms of their product.

Indeed from the classical theory, for example [
16
] Theorem 1.5.13, for any given
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
,
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
and
e_{i}
a basis vector of
\mathcal{H}
, there exists a unique continuous, adapted, bounded-variation process
[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]
with
[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]_{0}=0
(
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
) such that

\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]

is a real valued martingale. We would love to immediately have the existence and uniqueness of a corresponding
\mathcal{H}
valued process which gives a martingale when subtracted from
\bm{\Psi}Y
, but that isn’t clear in the same way that the quadratic variation was as in that case
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
was a genuine real valued submartingale. Our approach, therefore, comes from the characterisation in Proposition
1.5.11
. We make a first definition for the projected process.

Definition 1.5.12
.

For
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
, for any
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, we define the cross-variation process
[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]
by

[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]e_{i}.

Let’s take a moment to process this definition, particularly in terms of how we would like to define the cross-variation process. It falls from the corresponding properties of the real valued cross-variations that
[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]
is continuous, adapted and of bounded-variation (one can apply the triangle inequality for the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}})
satisfying
[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{0}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. In addition observe that

(\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi})Y-[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right)e_{i}

which we hope to be an
\mathcal{H}
valued martingale. To show this consider arbitrary
\phi\in\mathcal{H}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\langle(\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi})Y-[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y],\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right)e_{i},\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\phi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\left(\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right)e_{i},\left\langle\phi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right)\left\langle\phi,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

where we recall that each
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]
is a martingale, hence too is

\left(\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right)\left\langle\phi,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
and therefore the finite sum is a martingale. To comment on the uniqueness, we introduce a characterisation of indistinguishability in Hilbert Spaces.

Lemma 1.5.13
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}
and
\bm{\Phi}
be
\mathcal{H}
valued processes. Then
\bm{\Psi}
is indistinguishable from
\bm{\Phi}
if and only if for every basis vector
e_{i}
,
\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is indistinguishable from
\left\langle\bm{\Phi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

The first implication is trivial so we consider only the reverse one. That is, assume that for every
e_{i}
there exists a set
A_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
with
\mathbbm{P}(A_{i})=1
and for all
t\geq 0
and
\omega\in A_{i}
,

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle\bm{\Phi}_{t}(\omega),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

We now define
A:=\bigcap_{i}A_{i}
, which is again of full probability and in
\mathcal{F}
, and for any
\omega\in A
,
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)-\bm{\Phi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)-\bm{\Phi}_{t}(\omega),e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=0

which completes the proof.
∎

Armed with this lemma we consider the uniqueness of
[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]
. Suppose that
\Pi
is an
\mathcal{H}
valued process which continuous, adapted and of bounded-variation satisfying
\Pi_{0}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. Moreover suppose that

(\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi})Y-\Pi

is an
\mathcal{H}
valued martingale. Take any basis vector
e_{j}
. Then

\left\langle(\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi})Y-\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

is a real valued martingale, but this is just

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle e_{i},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(30)

There are two cases here:
j\leq k
and
j>k
. In the latter case then we have that
\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is a martingale. In both cases from the regularity of
\Pi
we have again that
\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is continuous, adapted and of bounded-variation satisfying
\left\langle\Pi_{0},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. In the real valued case it is classical that a martingale of bounded-variation must be constant, hence
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for all
t\geq 0
we have that
\left\langle\Pi_{t},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0.
Therefore
\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is indistinguishable from
\left\langle[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y],e_{j}\right\rangle
which is similarly zero (recall Definition
1.5.12
).

In the alternative case
j\leq k
, the real valued martingale (
30
) is given by

\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

and from the much discussed uniqueness in this setting we have that
\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is indistinguishable from
[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]
which is simply
\left\langle[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y],e_{k}\right\rangle
from its definition. Combining with Lemma
1.5.13
, what we have proved is the following.

Proposition 1.5.14
.

For
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
, for any
k\in\mathbbm{N}
,
[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]
is the unique continuous, adapted, bounded-variation
\mathcal{H}
valued process satisfying
[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{0}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
such that

(\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi})Y-[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]

is an
\mathcal{H}
valued martingale.

It remains for us to define the cross-variation process
[\bm{\Psi},Y]
. Referencing [
16
] Problem 1.5.7 for example, we know that
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for any
t\geq 0
,

[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]_{t}^{2}\leq[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]_{t}[Y]_{t}

hence

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left([\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]_{t}[Y]_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]_{t}\right)\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}[Y]_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]_{T}[Y]_{T}.

Moreover for
j<k
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}-[\mathcal{P}_{j}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}e_{i}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]_{T}[Y]_{T}

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi}-\mathcal{P}_{j}\bm{\Psi}]_{T}[Y]_{T}

referring to (
27
) and (
28
) for the last line. We look to follow a similar approach to Proposition
1.5.11
, taking the expectation. To do this we introduce the localising times

\tau_{n}:=n\wedge\inf\left\{0\leq t<\infty:[Y]_{t}\geq n\right\}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}-[\mathcal{P}_{j}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}-[\mathcal{P}_{j}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbbm{E}\left([\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi}-\mathcal{P}_{j}\bm{\Psi}]_{T}[Y]_{T}\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq n\mathbbm{E}\left([\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi}-\mathcal{P}_{j}\bm{\Psi}]_{T}\right)

which was shown to approach zero as
j\rightarrow\infty
, uniformly in
k
, in Proposition
1.5.11
. We have thus demonstrated that for every
n\in\mathbbm{N}
and
T\geq 0
, the sequence
\left([\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{n}}\right)
is Cauchy in the Banach Space
L^{2}\left(\Omega;L^{\infty}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)\right)
. We can therefore extract a subsequence which converges
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
. To remove the truncation we introduce the sets

A_{n}:=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\tau_{n}(\omega)\geq T\right\}.

Then on every
A_{n}
, there exists a subsequence of
\left([\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]\right)
which is
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
(within
A_{n}
) convergent in
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
. It should be noted that the choice of subsequence may be dependent on
n
, hence the separation, and also that this convergence is now of continuous processes so can be taken in
C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
. We look to upgrade this convergence to be of the whole sequence, by another Cauchy argument. Fix any
\varepsilon>0
, and we look to show the existence of a
J\in\mathbbm{N}
such that for all
k\geq J
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|[\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}-[\mathcal{P}_{J}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}<\varepsilon

or equivalently, as already shown in the proof,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=J+1}^{k}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}^{2}<\varepsilon.

Let the subsequence be indexed by
k_{m}
. The subsequence is convergent hence Cauchy, so there exists a
J_{m}
such that for all
k_{m}>J_{m}
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|[\mathcal{P}_{k_{m}}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}-[\mathcal{P}_{J_{m}}\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}<\varepsilon

or equivalently

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=J_{m}+1}^{k_{m}}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}^{2}<\varepsilon.

We now set
J:=J_{m}
and argue that for every
k>J
there exists a
k_{m}>k
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=J+1}^{k}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}^{2}\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=J+1}^{k_{m}}\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y\right]_{t}^{2}<\varepsilon

which proves the Cauchy property. Hence on each
A_{n}
, the sequence
([\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y])
is
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergent in
C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
. From the fact that
\mathbbm{P}\left(\bigcup_{n}A_{n}\right)=1
, we obtain the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergence on
\Omega
.

Definition 1.5.15
.

For
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
, we define the cross-variation process
[\bm{\Psi},Y]
by

[\bm{\Psi},Y]:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]e_{i}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for the limit taken in
C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
.

Our next question is then very natural: do we have the corresponding characterisation as in Proposition
1.5.14
? The fact that this cross-variation is continuous, adapted and starting from zero is proven as in Proposition
1.5.11
. In fact the martingality of

\bm{\Psi}Y-[\bm{\Psi},Y]

(31)

is again proven near identically, as we use that

(\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi})Y-\sum_{i=1}^{k}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]e_{i}

is a martingale, and the convergence of this at each
t\geq 0
in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
to (
31
).
It is the bounded-variation which proves problematic. Perhaps the most logical approach is to show that the finite sums are of uniformly bounded total variation, through an argument like

V^{T}_{\mathcal{H}}\left([\mathcal{P}_{k}\bm{\Psi},Y]\right)=V^{T}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]e_{i}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}V^{T}_{\mathcal{H}}\left([\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]e_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}V^{T}_{\mathbb{R}}\left([\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right).

Then from the real valued theory, see [
16
] Problem 1.5.7, we have that

V^{T}_{\mathbb{R}}\left([\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\left([\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]_{T}+[Y]_{T}\right)

but this means taking
\sum_{i=1}^{k}[Y]_{T}
which will explode as
k\rightarrow\infty
. In lieu of this bounded-variation, we do of course have the weaker property that every projection is of bounded variation. In fact, this is enough for uniqueness.

Suppose that
\Pi
is an
\mathcal{H}
valued process which is continuous, adapted, satisfying
\Pi_{0}=0
and such that for every basis vector
e_{j}
,
\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle
is of bounded-variation
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. Moreover suppose that

\bm{\Psi}Y-\Pi

is an
\mathcal{H}
valued martingale. Take any basis vector
e_{j}
. Then

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}Y-\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

is a real valued martingale, but this is just

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle e_{i},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

or simply

\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}Y-\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

Thus
\left\langle\Pi,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is indistinguishable from
[\left\langle\bm{\Psi},e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},Y]
which is simply
\left\langle[\bm{\Psi},Y],e_{k}\right\rangle
from its definition. Combining with Lemma
1.5.13
we deduce the uniqueness in this class, so have proven the following.

Proposition 1.5.16
.

For
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
,
[\bm{\Psi},Y]
is the unique continuous, adapted
\mathcal{H}
valued process satisfying
[\bm{\Psi},Y]_{0}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
such that for every basis vector
e_{j}
,
\left\langle[\bm{\Psi},Y],e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is of bounded-variation
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
and

\bm{\Psi}Y-[\bm{\Psi},Y]

is an
\mathcal{H}
valued martingale.

Lemma 1.5.17
.

Suppose that
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is a sequence of martingales in
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
which at every time
t\geq 0
, converges in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
to some
\bm{\Psi}_{t}
. Let
Y\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
. Suppose in addition that at any time
t\geq 0
, the sequence
\left([\bm{\Psi}^{n},Y]_{t}\right)
converges to some
y_{t}
in
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
where
y
is continuous, adapted and for every basis vector
e_{j}
,
\left\langle y,e_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is of bounded variation
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. Then
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
and
[\bm{\Psi},Y]
is indistinguishable from
y
.

Proof.

The proof is, unsurprisingly, similar to that of Lemma
1.5.10
. For each
n
the martingale in question is the
\mathcal{H}
valued one

\bm{\Psi}^{n}Y-[\bm{\Psi}^{n},Y].

We use again that the
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
limit preserves martingality and that by Hölder’s inequality the
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
limit of
\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}Y_{t}
is
\bm{\Psi}_{t}Y_{t}
. With the same arguments as in Lemma
1.5.10
we conclude the proof.
∎

If the given processes were only (continuous) local martingales, then we can make a slightly modified version of the definition. Assuming without loss of generality that
\bm{\Psi}
and
Y
are locally square integrable (see the discussion after Definitionn
1.3.3
), localised by stopping times
(R_{n})
and
(T_{n})
respectively, then for a new sequence of stopping times defined by

\tau_{n}=R_{n}\wedge T_{n}

the stopped processes
\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}
and
Y^{\tau_{n}}
are genuine square integrable martingales (in their respective spaces), so the cross variation
[\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}},Y^{\tau_{n}}]
can be defined. The canonical localisation procedure is evident once more, as the
(\tau_{n})
go to infinity almost surely, the consistency conditions that for
m\leq n
and
t\leq\tau_{m}
we have

\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{m}}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}_{t}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad Y^{\tau_{m}}_{t}=Y^{\tau_{n}}_{t}

allow us once more to define the process at almost every
\omega
and any
t
by

\Big([\bm{\Psi},Y]_{t}\Big)(\omega):=\Big([\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}},Y^{\tau_{n}}]_{t}\Big)(\omega)

(32)

for any
n
such that
t\leq\tau_{n}(\omega)
, independently of this choice of
n
. Then the process

\bm{\Psi}Y-[\bm{\Psi},Y]

is itself a local martingale, localised by the stopping times
(\tau_{n})
. The argument justifying this is identical to Proposition
1.5.6
, from which it is similarly clear that

[\bm{\Psi},Y]^{\tau_{n}}=[\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}},Y^{\tau_{n}}].

In the traditional way, these notions can all be extended to semi-martingales (that is, a martingale plus a bounded-variation process). The quadratic and cross variation of such semi-martingales is then simply the quadratric/cross variation of the corresponding martingale parts. To this end we introduce the notation
\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}
and
\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
to be the corresponding spaces of square integrable continuous semi-martingales, and similarly
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{c},\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
to be the spaces of continuous semi-martingales.

1.6
Integration Driven by Cylindrical Brownian Motion

For our analysis now we will need to make reference to two distinct Hilbert Spaces; one over which
\mathcal{W}
is a Cylindrical Brownian Motion, and the other in which our integrand maps to. Henceforth we introduce
\mathfrak{U}
as the Hilbert Space over which
\mathcal{W}
is a Cylindrical Brownian Motion. We shall take
(e_{i})
as an orthonormal basis over
\mathfrak{U}
and
(a_{i})
an orthonormal basis over
\mathcal{H}
.

Definition 1.6.1
.

Denote by
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
the class of progressively measurable operator valued processes
B
belonging to the set
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})\big).
Measurability here is again defined with respect to the Borel Sigma algebra on
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H}).

Note that we make no explicit reference to
\mathfrak{U}
, the space on which
\mathcal{W}
is a cylindrical Brownian Motion. This is because, in practice, the space
\mathfrak{U}
will be arbitrarily chosen; this shall be discussed later.

Definition 1.6.2
.

The class of processes
B
such that
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
for all
T
will be denoted by
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W}).

Recall from Subsection
1.4
that if
\mathcal{W}
is a Cylindrical Brownian motion over
\mathfrak{U}
, it can be formally represented by

\mathcal{W}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}e_{i}W^{i}_{t}

(33)

where the
(W^{i})
are standard independent one-dimensional Brownian motions.

Definition 1.6.3
.

For
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
we define the Itô stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(34)

as the
\mathcal{H}
valued random variable

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}

(35)

where each integral is defined as in Definition
1.2.6
and the infinite sum is taken in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big).

The immediate response to this definition is to prove that
(\ref{projectedintegral})
is well defined; that is the integrals are well defined, as is the limit. Firstly for each
i
,
B_{e_{i}}
is trivially in
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{H}\big)
as this norm is bounded by the
L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})\big)
norm of
B
. The progressive measurability is inherited from that of
B
.

In order to show that the limit of partial sums is well defined, we proceed similarly to the method applied for (
19
) and argue that the sequence of partial sums is Cauchy. Observe that

\displaystyle\Big|\Big|\sum_{i=m}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}\Big|\Big|^{2}_{L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\Big|\Big|\sum_{i=m}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}\Big|\Big|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=m}^{n}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds

having applied the Itô Isometry
1.2.9
to the above. But by assumption that
B\in L^{2}\big(\Omega\times[0,t];\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})\big)
we know

\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds<\infty

and thus, by Tonelli’s theorem regarding the infinite sum as an integral with respect to the counting measure,

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds<\infty

demonstrating the required Cauchy property. Of course the
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
norm of the limit is the limit of the
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
norms, so we have justified the following.

Proposition 1.6.4
.

For
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
, we have

\mathbbm{E}\left(\left\|\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)=\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}ds\right).

It is worth noting that whilst we impose the condition

\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds<\infty

one may instead require the looser condition

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}ds<\infty\qquad\mathbbm{P}-a.s.

(36)

or equivalently that
B:\Omega\rightarrow L^{2}\big([0,t];\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})\big)
for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
. Our formulation follows the classical construction as laid out in Subsection
1.2
, ensuring that the integral is a genuine square integrable martingale. We can just as straightforwardly follow the arguments from Definition
1.3.3
, which are laid out here.

Definition 1.6.5
.

Denote by
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
the class of progressively measurable operator valued processes
B
such that
B(\omega)
belongs to the set
L^{2}\big([0,T];\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})\big)
for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
.

Definition 1.6.6
.

The class of processes
B
such that
B\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
for all
T
will be denoted by
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W}).

Using the template after Definition
1.3.3
, for a process
B\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
let’s introduce

\tau_{n}:=n\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}ds\geq n\}

taking the convention that the infimum of the empty set is infinite. The
(\tau_{n})
are stopping times as they are simply first hitting times of the continuous and adapted random variable
\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}ds.
These times tend to infinity
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
by condition (
36
). Now define the truncated processes
B^{n}
as

B^{n}(t):=B(t)\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}

and using the fact that for
m\leq n
, and
t\leq\tau_{m}
, we have

B(t)\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{n}}=B(t)\mathbbm{1}_{t\leq\tau_{m}}

we can make the following consistent definition.

Definition 1.6.7
.

In the setting described, we define

\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega):=\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B^{n}(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega)

(37)

at
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
for any
n
such that
t\leq\tau_{n}(\omega)
, noting that such an
n
exists from the assumed condition (
36
).

The justification of this definition is identical to that discussed in Subsection
1.3
, such that for this fixed
n
we have
B^{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
and subsequently the complete definition

\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega):=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}dW^{i}_{s}\Big)(\omega)

(38)

with the limit again in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
. To be clear this is a limit averaging over all
\Omega
which is taken for the fixed
n
, which was chosen with respect to the specific
\omega
in which we are evaluating the limit. We will have no quarrels in writing (
37
) as a formal expression

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}

(39)

motivated by the fact that as
B_{e_{i}}\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}}
(
1.3.4
) then by definition

\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}dW^{i}_{s}

at this same choice of
\omega
, for the same fixed
n
. We say the expression is only formal though, as the infinite sum in (
39
) is
not
the
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
limit of the partial sums of the local martingales as presented. We understand (
39
) only by (
38
), that is by choosing the
\omega
at which we evaluate (
39
), then fixing our
n
associated to this
\omega
, before then taking the limit in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
of the genuine square integrable martingales (given by stopping the local martingales at
\tau_{n}
) which is finally then evaluated at
\omega
.

We now look to show a series of properties of this integral which were shown for a one dimensional Brownian Motion across the earlier subsections. The first is the corresponding result of Theorem
1.2.10
.

Theorem 1.6.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
are Hilbert spaces such that
B\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}_{1}}(\mathcal{W})
and
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1};\mathcal{H}_{2})
. Then the process
TB
defined by

TB_{e_{i}}(s,\omega)=T\big(B_{e_{i}}(s,\omega)\big)

belongs to
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}_{2}}(\mathcal{W})
and is such that

T\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)=\int_{0}^{t}TB(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

In addition, the two integrals are defined pointwise
a.e.
with respect to the same stopping times.

Proof.

We shall prove first that
TB\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}_{2}}(\mathcal{W})
. The progressive measurability is preserved under the continuity of
T
, and for
C
the (square of the) boundedness constant associated to
T
we have

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert TB_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}ds\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}ds<\infty

holding
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
as
B\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}_{1}}(\mathcal{W})
. In addition for any stopping time
\tau_{n}
as in Definition
1.6.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\big(TB_{e_{i}}(s)\big)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}ds\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert T\big(B_{e_{i}}(s)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\big)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}ds\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbbm{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert B_{e_{i}}(s)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}ds\Big)

\displaystyle<\infty

so the new stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{t}TB(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}

can be constructed using the same sequence of stopping times. We will freely use linearity of
T
to commute it with the indicator function, not showing this explicitly with brackets. To carry
T
through the integral however let’s avoid danger and write out explicitly what we want to show, which is for almost any choice of
\omega
with the associated
\tau_{n}
and fixed
n
as in Definition
1.6.7
, then

T\bigg(\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega)\bigg):=T\bigg(\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}^{n}(s)dW^{i}_{s}\Big)(\omega)\bigg)=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}TB_{e_{i}}^{n}(s)dW^{i}_{s}\Big)(\omega)

where the left hand side limit is taken in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}_{1}\big)
and the right side in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}_{2}\big)
. From the
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}_{1}\big)
limit there exists a subsequence convergent almost everywhere in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
(we assume w.l.o.g that
\omega
belongs to this set of full probability). Working with this subsequence, we can pass the
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
limit through the continuous
T
such that it is now the
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
limit in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Linearity of
T
allows us to hit each term in the sum individually, so we have now that

T\bigg(\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega)\bigg)=\bigg(\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}T\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}^{n}(s)dW^{i}_{s}\Big)\bigg)(\omega)

(40)

for the limit
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
of the subsequence indexed by
(n_{k})
. Applying Theorem
1.2.10
, we can commute
T
with the integral on the right side of (
40
). However we have justified already that the limit over the whole sequence in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}_{2}\big)
exists, agreeing with the
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}_{2}\big)
limit of the subsequence, which in turn agrees with the
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
limit. Thus by definition of the stochastic integral, (
40
) is simply the equality

T\bigg(\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega)\bigg)=\Big(\int_{0}^{t}TB(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)(\omega)

completing the proof.

∎

This is stated in the more general form for a process only in
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{I}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathcal{H}_{1}}(\mathcal{W})
, but from the proof the following is clear.

Corollary 1.6.8.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
are Hilbert spaces such that
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{1}}(\mathcal{W})
and
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1};\mathcal{H}_{2})
. Then the process
TB
defined by

TB_{e_{i}}(s,\omega)=T\big(B_{e_{i}}(s,\omega)\big)

belongs to
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}_{2}}(\mathcal{W})
and is such that

T\Big(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}\Big)=\int_{0}^{t}TB(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

Proposition 1.6.9
.

Let
B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
and
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. Then for every
t>0
we have that

\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

(41)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
.

Proof.

Firstly we make clear that
\left\langle B,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is understood as a process defined by the mapping

e_{i}\times\omega\times t\rightarrow\left\langle B_{t}(e_{i},\omega),\phi(\omega)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

The fact that
\left\langle B,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W})
is completely analogous to Proposition
1.3.5
, where the progressive measurability follows as the mapping

\left\langle B_{\cdot},\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:t\times\omega\times\tilde{\omega}\rightarrow\left\langle B_{t}(\omega),\phi(\tilde{\omega})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

is
\mathcal{B}([0,T])\times\mathcal{F}_{T}\times\mathcal{F}_{0}
measurable as a mapping into
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})
. Similarly we have that

\left\|\left\langle B_{t}(\omega),\phi(\omega)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right\|_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})}\leq\lVert\phi(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert B_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}

which is sufficient to justify that
\left\langle B,\phi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W})
. The extension of Proposition
1.3.7
to this result is then identical to the extension of Theorem
1.2.10
to
1.6.8
, so we conclude the proof here.

∎

Proposition 1.6.10
.

Let
B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
and
\eta:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. Then
\eta B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
and for every
t>0
we have that

\eta\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}\eta B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
.

Proof.

The proof follows identically to Proposition
1.6.9
in analogy with Proposition
1.3.8
. We make explicit that
\eta B
is defined by the mapping

e_{i}\times\omega\times t\rightarrow\eta(\omega)B_{t}(e_{i},\omega).

∎

Remark 1
.

Although we have not explicitly addressed the construction of the integral over a time interval
[s,t]
where
s>0
, this can be done without any extra difficulty just as in the standard real valued case. If we were to just consider the integral over
[s,t]
in Proposition
1.6.9
, then the results extends to any
\mathcal{F}_{s}-
measurable
\phi,\eta
in Propositions
1.6.9
,
1.6.10
. To show this we of course revisit Proposition
1.3.5
, appreciating that the
\mathcal{F}_{s}-
measurability does not disturb the measurability requirements of the simple process.

We also extend the Stochastic Dominated Convergence Theorem to this setting.

Lemma 1.6.11
.

Let
(B^{n})
be a sequence in
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
such that there exists processes
B:\Omega\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})
and
Q\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
with the properties that for every
T>0
,
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.e.

(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T]
:

1.

\lVert B^{n}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}\leq\lVert Q_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}
for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}
;

2.

(B^{n}_{t}(\omega))
is convergent to
B_{t}(\omega)
in
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})
.

Then
B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
and for every
t>0
, there exists a subsequence indexed by
(n_{k})
such that

\lim_{n_{k}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}B^{n_{k}}_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

(42)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
.

Proof.

The proof is mechanically identical to that of Lemma
1.3.6
, simply replacing
\mathcal{H}
by
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})
when dealing with the integrands and using the appropriate Itô Isometry
1.6.4
.
∎

We now shift attentions to results regarding the martingale properties of the integral.

Proposition 1.6.12
.

For
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
, the Itô stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}

belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H}).

Proof.

This follows immediately from Propositions
1.5.4
and
1.5.5
.
∎

Corollary 1.6.12.1
.

For
B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
, the Itô stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}

is a continuous local martingale.

Proof.

This now follows identically to Proposition
1.5.6
.
∎

The martingality of the integral also allows us to consider the quadratic variation as defined in Definition
1.5.7
.

Proposition 1.6.13
.

For
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
, we have that

\left[\int_{0}^{\cdot}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right]_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{r}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}dr.

(43)

Proof.

At each time
t
, the integral

\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

is defined to be the
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
limit of the sequence

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r}.

We look to infer the quadratic variation of this sequence of processes using Proposition
1.5.8
, to then apply Lemma
1.5.10
. We claim that

\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\cdot}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r}\right]_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr

which is to say

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr

(44)

is a martingale. For the orthonormal basis
(a_{k})
of
\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{j})dW^{j}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i\neq j}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{j})dW^{j}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\left\|\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i\neq j}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{j})dW^{j}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right).

Inspecting the last equality, by Proposition
1.5.8
we have that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\left\|\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\right)

is a finite sum of martingales, so we would prove that the process defined in (
44
) also belongs to this class if we show that the same is true of

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i\neq j}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{i})dW^{i}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}(e_{j})dW^{j}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(45)

We consider the above for each fixed
k
, rewriting it as

\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right).

(46)

Adaptedness of this process is clear, and to show integrability observe that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left|\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbbm{E}\left|\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbbm{E}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\mathbbm{E}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right)^{1/2}\left(\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq n^{2}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq n^{2}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad<\infty.

As for the martingale property, for any times
s<t
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right)\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=0

(47)

where passage from the first line to the second is through the independent increments property of the Brownian Motions, and the second to the third is from the independence of the Brownian Motions.
So the process defined in (
46
) is shown to be a martingale, where we wish to show that this property remains true in the limit of the infinite sum for (
45
). Convergence of the infinite sum is defined
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
, and it is sufficient to show that the convergence also holds in
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
. For this we show that the sequence is Cauchy in
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
, taking the difference of the
l^{th}
and
m^{th}
terms to see that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left|\sum_{k=m+1}^{l}\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{k=m+1}^{l}\mathbbm{E}\left|\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{j}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{j}_{r}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{r}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq n^{2}\sum_{k=m+1}^{l}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq n^{2}\sum_{k=m+1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr

which is a monotone decreasing sequence to zero in
m
, hence the Cauchy property is shown so there exists a limit in
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
which must agree with the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
limit (we can take a
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergent subsequence from the
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
convergence) and the martingale property of the process defined in (
45
) and hence (
44
) is shown. We thus apply Lemma
1.5.10
and deduce that
\left[\int_{0}^{\cdot}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right]_{t}
is the
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
limit of the sequence

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert B_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr

in
n
. Similarly to the analysis just conducted we can show that this sequence is Cauchy in
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
and agrees with the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
limit, which is of course

\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{r}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}dr

taking the infinite sum through the integral with either Tonelli’s Theorem (identifying the infinite sum as a integral with respect to the counting measure) or the Monotone Convergence Theorem. The proof is concluded.
∎

We also have the analogous result to Proposition
1.5.9
.

Proposition 1.6.14
.

Let
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{T}(\mathcal{W})
and consider any sequence of partitions

I_{l}:=\left\{0=t^{l}_{0}<t^{l}_{1}<\dots<t^{l}_{k_{l}}=T\right\}

with
\max_{j}|t^{l}_{j}-t^{l}_{j-1}|\rightarrow 0
as
l\rightarrow\infty
. Then for all
t\in[0,T]
, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{r}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}dr\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

(48)

Proof.

Following the method used in Proposition
1.5.9
we again would like to reduce this to a familiar case and extrapolate the result to the limit. We introduce a sequence of stopping times
(\tau^{n})
defined at every
n\in\mathbbm{N}
by

\tau^{n}:=n\wedge\inf\left\{t\in[0,T]:\left\|\int_{0}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{r}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}dr\geq n\right\}.

For every
n
we define the process

B^{n}_{\cdot}:=B_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau^{n}}

and now look to show that

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B^{n}_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B^{n}_{r}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}dr\right|\right)=0.

(49)

This is precisely in line with the method of Proposition
1.5.9
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B^{n}_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B^{n}_{r}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B^{n}_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert B^{n}_{r}(e_{i})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B^{n}_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\langle B^{n}_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle B^{n}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle B^{n}_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle B^{n}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lVert\left\langle B^{n}_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})}^{2}dr\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle B^{n}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\left\langle B^{n}_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})}^{2}dr\right|\right)

having applied Theorem
1.6.8
and the Dominated Convergence Theorem to take the infinite sum in
k
through the time integral and expectation. From Theorem
1.6.8
and Proposition
1.6.12
then
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\left\langle B^{n}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{c}
, with quadratic variation
\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\left\langle B_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})}^{2}dr
coming from Proposition
1.6.13
. Just as we used in Proposition
1.5.9
, we have that for each fixed
k\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\sum_{t^{l}_{j+1}\leq t}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle B^{n}_{r},a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\left\langle B^{n}_{r}(e_{i}),a_{k}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})}^{2}dr\right|\right)=0

so it is sufficient to justify the interchange of limit in
l
and summation in
k
. This follows identically to the justification in Proposition
1.5.9
, appealing this time to the Itô Isometry
1.6.4
.
∎

2
Stochastic Differential Equations in Infinite Dimensions

Throughout this section we shall again use
(e_{i})
as an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{U}
, the space over which
\mathcal{W}
is a Cylindrical Brownian Motion (recall (
33
)), and
(a_{k})
an orthonormal basis of the Hilbert Space in which the equation takes place.

2.1
The Stratonovich Integral

We look at first to define the Stratonovich Integral with respect to a one dimensional martingale, before then doing with respect to a Cylindrical Brownian Motion.

Definition 2.1.1
.

For
M\in\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}
and
\bm{\Psi}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\cap\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
, the Stratonovich stochastic integral is defined as

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\circ dM_{s}:=\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dM_{s}+\frac{1}{2}[\bm{\Psi},M]_{t}.

Definition 2.1.2
.

For
B\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
such that
B_{e_{i}}\in\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}({\mathcal{H}})
for every
e_{i}
and the limit

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[B_{e_{i}},W^{i}]_{t}

is well defined in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
, the Stratnovich stochastic integral is defined as

\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{t}B_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}[B_{e_{i}},W^{i}]_{t}\right)

where the limit is taken in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
. The class of such processes will be denoted
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\circ}(\mathcal{W}).

It will be necessary to extend this definition to processes
B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
, but we encounter more technical issues in trying to construct a sequence of stopping times such that the stopped process belongs to
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\circ}(\mathcal{W})
. We find it simplest to give the definition below.

Definition 2.1.3
.

Suppose that there exists a sequence of stopping times
(\tau_{n})
which are
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
monotone increasing and convergent to infinity such that:

1.

For every
n
, the process

B^{n}(\cdot):=B({\cdot})\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau^{n}}

belongs to
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
;

2.

For every
n
and
i
, the process

B^{\tau_{n}}_{e_{i}}(\cdot):=B_{e_{i}}(\cdot\wedge\tau^{n})

belongs to
\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{H})
;

3.

The limit

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[B^{\tau_{n}}_{e_{i}},W^{i}]_{t}

is well defined in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
.

Then the Stratonovich stochastic integral is defined at a fixed
\omega
for any
\tau^{n}(\omega)\geq t
as

\left(\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right)(\omega):=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{t}B^{n}_{e_{i}}(s)dW^{i}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}[B^{\tau_{n}}_{e_{i}},W^{i}]_{t}\right)\right)(\omega)

where the limit is taken in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
. The class of such processes will be denoted
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\circ}(\mathcal{W}).

This definition is of course completely analogous to the localisation procedure used in the previous constructions, except we postulate in the first instance the existence of the localising sequence
(\tau_{n})
.

2.2
Strong Solutions in the Abstract Framework

We work with a quartet of embedded Hilbert Spaces

V\hookrightarrow H\hookrightarrow U\hookrightarrow X

where the embedding is meant as a continuous linear injection. We introduce at first the Itô SPDE

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(50)

where
\mathcal{W}
continues to be a Cylindrical Brownian Motion over
\mathfrak{U}
relative to our fixed filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t}),\mathbbm{P})
with representation (
33
). We impose now some conditions on the operators relative to these spaces. To do so we define the general operator
\tilde{K}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\tilde{K}(\phi):=c\left(1+\lVert\phi\rVert_{U}^{p}+\lVert\phi\rVert_{H}^{q}\right)

for any constants
c,p,q
independent of
\phi
.

Assumption 2.2.1
.

\mathcal{Q}:V\rightarrow U
is measurable and for any
\phi\in V
,

\lVert\mathcal{Q}\phi\rVert_{U}\leq\tilde{K}(\phi)[1+\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}^{2}].

Assumption 2.2.2
.

\mathcal{G}
is understood as a measurable operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle:V\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle:H\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};U)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle:U\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};X)

defined over
\mathfrak{U}
by its action on the basis vectors

\mathcal{G}(\cdot,e_{i}):=\mathcal{G}_{i}(\cdot).

Each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is linear and there exists constants
c_{i}
such that for all
\phi\in V
,
\psi\in H
,
\eta\in U
:

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle\leq c_{i}\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\psi\rVert_{U}

\displaystyle\leq c_{i}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\eta\rVert_{X}

\displaystyle\leq c_{i}\lVert\eta\rVert_{U}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle<\infty.

It is worth clarifying how
\mathcal{G}
is defined over
\mathfrak{U}
: fix a
\phi\in V
and consider
\mathcal{G}(\phi,\cdot):\mathfrak{U}\rightarrow H
(the arguments here apply for the larger spaces as well). Any
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
has the representation

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\alpha,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{U}}e_{i}

where

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\alpha,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{U}}^{2}<\infty.

Then

\mathcal{G}(\phi,\cdot):\alpha\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\alpha,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi

is well defined as an element of
H
. This is justified by showing that the sequence of partial sums is Cauchy in
H
: note that from Cauchy-Schwarz,

\left\|\sum_{i=m}^{n}\left\langle\alpha,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi\right\|_{H}^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{i=m}^{n}\left\langle\alpha,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{U}}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=m}^{n}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi\rVert^{2}_{H}\right)\leq\left(\sum_{i=m}^{\infty}\left\langle\alpha,e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{U}}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=m}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}^{2}\right)

which approaches zero as
m\rightarrow\infty
as the sums are finite. We introduce now the first notion of our strong solutions.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow H
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. A pair
(\bm{\Psi},\tau)
where
\tau
is a
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
positive stopping time and
\bm{\Psi}
is a process such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];H\right)
and
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)}\in L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for all
T>0
with
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
progressively measurable in
V
, is said to be a local strong solution of the equation (
50
) if the identity

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(51)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\geq 0
.

Let’s take a few moments to process this definition and ensure that the integrals make sense with the given regularity of the solution and the operators
\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{G}
. As an initial aside note that if
(\bm{\Psi},\tau)
is a
V
-valued local strong solution of the equation (
50
), then
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}=\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot\wedge\tau}
. Moreover the progressive measurability condition on
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
may look a little suspect as
\bm{\Psi}_{0}
itself may only belong to
H
and not
V
making it impossible for
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
to be even adapted in
V
. We are mildly abusing notation here; what we really ask is that there exists a process
\bm{\Phi}
which is progressively measurable in
V
and such that
\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}=\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
almost surely over the product space
\Omega\times[0,T]
for every
T\geq 0
with product measure
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda
.

The time integral in (
51
) is well defined in
U
as a Bochner Integral: first of all
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)}\in L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
so
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)}:[0,t]\rightarrow V
is measurable hence
\mathcal{Q}(\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega))}:[0,t]\rightarrow U
is measurable from Assumption
2.2.1
. Moreover the mapping
\mathcal{Q}(\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)})\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)}:[0,t]\rightarrow U
is again measurable and we have that

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau(\omega)}\mathcal{Q}\left(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\right)ds=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\left(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau(\omega)}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau(\omega)}ds

so the required measurability in order to define the integral is satisfied. In this vein we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau(\omega)}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\left(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\right)\rVert_{U}ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau(\omega)}\tilde{K}\left(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\right)\left[1+\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\rVert_{V}^{2}\right]ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\tau(\omega)]}\left[\tilde{K}\left(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\right)\right]\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau(\omega)}1+\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\rVert_{V}^{2}ds

\displaystyle<\infty

employing Assumption
2.2.1
again and using the regularity specified by the local solution to deduce finiteness, which justifies that the integral is well defined.

As for the stochastic integral in (
51
), this is well defined in the sense of Definition
1.6.7
in
H
(which then embeds into
U
), though it is done so formally via the process
\bm{\Phi}
stipulated in the above remark regarding progressive measurability. We understand again that

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}(\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau})\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

should the integral be well defined, and so we actually define the integral by

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}:=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Phi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

The progressive measurability of
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Phi}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}:[0,t]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)
is immediate from the measurability assumption of
2.2.2
, the same arguments above for the time integral and the progressive measurability of
\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
coming from the definition of the stopping time. Similarly we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\bm{\Phi}_{s}(\omega)\right)\rVert_{H}^{2}ds=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau}\right)\rVert_{H}^{2}ds<\infty

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
, using again Assumption
2.2.2
, validating that this term is well defined as a local martingale in
H
and thus in
U
from the continuous embedding. We make no claims that this is genuinely a square integrable martingale.

We treat the local solution here to deal with the additional technicalities which arise from accounting for the stopping time. Global solutions are similarly defined however.

Definition 2.2.4
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow H
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. A process
\bm{\Psi}
such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];H\right)\cap L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for all
T>0
with
\bm{\Psi}
progressively measurable in
V
, is said to be a strong solution of the equation (
50
) if the identity (
50
) holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\geq 0
.

2.3
Stratonovich SPDEs in the Abstract Framework

We work now with the same initial condition
\bm{\Psi}_{0}
and operators
\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{G}
but instead pose the question of how to understand the Stratonovich SPDE

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

(52)

We are slightly hesitant to define our strong solution for the equation (
52
) as understanding the Stratonovich integral is delicate given the necessary semi-martingale structure. If we can show that
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies an evolution equation of an Itô stochastic integral plus a time integral then we can deduce the required semi-martingality and identify the martingale part, but we would need this assumption on semi-martingality
a priori
to define the Stratonovich integral in the sense of
2.1.3
in order to show the desired representation. To this end, we identify a Stratonovich SPDE with an Itô one in the sense given here.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

If
\bm{\Psi}
is a strong solution of the equation

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right)ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(53)

then
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity (
52
)
\mathbbm{P}-

a.s.
in
X
for all
t\geq 0
.

There is a little to unpack here before going on to the proof of this result. The first is how we understand the infinite sum of (
53
) and subsequently the SPDE. The operator

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}:V\rightarrow U

is defined as the pointwise limit of the partial sums, which is well defined as for any fixed
\phi\in V
,

\left\|\sum_{i=m}^{n}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\phi\right\|_{U}\leq\sum_{i=m}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\phi\right\|_{U}\leq\sum_{i=m}^{n}c_{i}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi\right\|_{H}\leq\sum_{i=m}^{n}c_{i}^{2}\left\|\phi\right\|_{V}

(54)

which as seen before approaches zero as
m\rightarrow\infty
. To understand the strong solution as defined in Definition
2.2.4
we need to show that the new operator
\mathcal{Q}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}
satisfies the assumptions postulated in Assumption
2.2.1
, but this is clear as
\mathcal{G}_{i}:V\rightarrow H
and
\mathcal{G}_{i}:H\rightarrow U
are bounded linear hence continuous so measurable, thus too is
\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}:V\rightarrow U
and therefore the partial sums and the pointwise limit are as well. Moreover

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\phi\right\|_{U}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}

as seen in (
54
) so the boundedness is also satisfied so we can understand the SPDE (
53
) in the same manner as (
50
). It also remains to be checked that the Stratonovich integral of (
52
) is well defined for
\bm{\Psi}
a strong solution of (
53
). We show this in the sense of Definition
2.1.3
for
\mathcal{H}=X
, the space in which the identity is satisfied. We show that the sequence of stopping times

\tau_{n}:=n\wedge\inf\left\{s\geq 0:\int_{0}^{s}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}dr\geq n\right\}

fit the requirements of Definition
2.1.3
, noting immediately that the sequence is
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
monotone increasing and convergent to infinity as the process

s\mapsto\int_{0}^{s}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}dr

is continuous. Moreover the process
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}:=\mathcal{G}(\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{n}})=\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{n}}
(using linearity of
\mathcal{G}
) is progressively measurable in
H
(we remark again that this is really
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Phi}^{n}
as stipulated but we identify the two) and satisfies the bound

\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}\rVert_{H}^{2}dr\leq\mathbbm{E}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}n<\infty

freely applying Tonelli’s Theorem between the expectation, integral and sum. Thus
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{H}(\mathcal{W})
so the integral can be constructed in
H
and then embedded into
X
, but we note that the embedding
J:H\xrightarrow{}X
is a continuous linear operator and so from Corollary
1.6.8.1
then
J(\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n})\in\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathcal{W})
and in particular

J\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right)=\int_{0}^{t}J(\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}

so there is no ambiguity in how we understand the integral as an element of
X
. Indeed we simply make the identification
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}
with
J(\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n})
and will make no explicit reference to the embeddings in our analysis henceforth. As for showing that
\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}\in\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{c}(X)
, we look at the evolution equation satisfied by
\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}
which is

\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathcal{Q}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\right)(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s})\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
