, therefore from Corollary
1.6.8.1
we have that

\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}_{t}=\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\left(\mathcal{Q}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\right)(\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s})\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\right)ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(55)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
X
(
\mathcal{G}_{i}:U\rightarrow X
is bounded and linear). The time integral is of bounded-variation in
X
and from Proposition
1.6.12
we have the result. The last thing to prove here is that the infinite sum

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}},W^{i}]_{t}

(56)

converges in
L^{2}(\Omega;X)
. From the identity (
55
) and the definition of the cross-variation for the semi-martingale,

[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{\tau_{n}},W^{i}]_{t}=\left[\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s},W^{i}\right]_{t}.

(57)

We can now use Lemma
1.5.17
and the definition of the integral as an
L^{2}\left(\Omega;X\right)
limit to see that

\left[\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s},W^{i}\right]_{t}=\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW^{j}_{s},W^{i}\right]_{t}=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW^{j}_{s},W^{i}\right]_{t}

where the limit is taken in
L^{1}(\Omega,X)
, should this limit exist and satisfy the conditions of Lemma
1.5.17
.
We consider
(a_{k})
as an orthonormal basis of
X
, so recalling Definition
1.5.15
,

\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW^{j}_{s},W^{i}\right]_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left[\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW^{j}_{s},a_{k}\right\rangle_{X},W^{i}\right]_{t}a_{k}.

Now we can first use Theorem
1.2.8
to reduce this to

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},a_{k}\right\rangle_{X}dW^{j}_{s},W^{i}\right]_{t}a_{k}

from which the classical real valued theory informs us that for
m\geq i
, due to the independence of the Brownian Motions, this is simply

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},a_{k}\right\rangle_{X}ds\right)a_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds,a_{k}\right\rangle_{X}a_{k}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds.

Therefore the limit as
m\rightarrow\infty
is well defined and we have the representation for (
57
). The convergence of the infinite sum in
L^{2}(\Omega,X)
, (
56
), will follow from our standard Cauchy argument, though we have to work a little harder here. The Cauchy argument requires showing that

\mathbbm{E}\left\|\sum_{i=m}^{k}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds\right\|_{X}^{2}\longrightarrow 0

as
m,k\rightarrow\infty
to which end we note that

\mathbbm{E}\left\|\sum_{i=m}^{k}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds\right\|_{X}^{2}\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=m}^{k}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\right\|_{X}ds\right)^{2}

and

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=m}^{k}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\right\|_{X}ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=m}^{k}c_{i}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\right\|_{H}ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\sum_{i=m}^{k}c_{i}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}c\left\|\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\right\|_{V}ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(c\sum_{i=m}^{k}c_{i}^{2}\right)^{2}\mathbbm{E}\left[t\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\right\|_{V}^{2}ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(c\sum_{i=m}^{k}c_{i}^{2}\right)^{2}tn

where
c
is the constant from the embedding of
V\xhookrightarrow{}H
. As
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}<\infty
then the Cauchy property follows, hence the Stratonovich integral is indeed well defined.

Proof of
2.3.1
:

We must show that

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
X
for all
t\geq 0
. So working with fixed arbitrary
t
and
\omega
, we choose any
n
such that
\tau_{n}(\omega)\geq t
and have that at this
\omega
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n},W^{i}]_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n},W^{i}]_{t}

where the limit is taken in
L^{2}(\Omega;X)
. Of course we have just shown that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n},W^{i}]_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds

is well defined in this topology, but we must show that it is equal to

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds

evaluated at this fixed
\omega
, for the pointwise limit in
U
as it was defined. Firstly note that

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}_{s}(\omega)ds=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}(\omega)ds

and by an application of the Dominated Convergence Theorem with dominating function

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{2}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{V}

we can rewrite

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{2}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}ds

as a limit
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
, and thus
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
X
. However the convergence in
L^{2}(\Omega;X)
implies that of a subsequence
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
X
, which agrees with the limit of the whole sequence
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
X
thus giving the result.
∎

This theorem has been stated for the strong solution (Definition
2.2.4
), though we note that all arguments follow in the corresponding local case by incorporating the stopping time as done in the justification that the integrals in Definition
2.2.3
are well defined. The result is stated below.

Corollary 2.3.1.1
.

If
(\bm{\Psi},\tau)
is a local strong solution of the equation (
53
)
then
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-

a.s.
in
X
for all
t\geq 0
.

We should emphasise why the identity (
52
) holds only in
X
and not in
U
, the space in which (
53
) is satisfied. The evolution equation (
55
) allowed us to identify the semi-martingale structure in
X
as is this is where the integrals are constructed. We can, however, construct the stochastic integral in (
55
) in
U
, which allows us to conclude that the time integral is itself an element of
U
(as
\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t},\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{0}
are as well). Unfortunately we cannot say that this is actually an integral in
U
(just an integral in
X
which is in turn an element of
U
) so poignantly we cannot say that this is of bounded-variation in
U
, which would be necessary when considering the cross-variation
[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n},W^{i}]_{t}
in
U
.

We use Theorem
2.3.1
as a way of defining the Stratonovich SPDE, however as discussed, with a priori martingality assumptions then the Stratonovich integral is well defined and one can prove a converse of this theorem.

Theorem 2.3.2
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}
be such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];H\right)\cap L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for all
T>0
with
\bm{\Psi}
progressively measurable in
V
. Assume in addition that
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{U}_{\circ}(\mathcal{W})
and
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity (
52
)
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\geq 0
. Then
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity (
53
)
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
X
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

By assumption the Stratonovich integral is well defined so we can write

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi},W^{i}\right]_{t}

for the cross variation taken in
U
. We wish to write out this cross variation process explicitly, so as in the proof of Theorem
2.3.1
we consider the evolution equation satisfied by
\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}
. It is the cross-variation term which could be problematic, but we appreciate that

\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]_{t}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]_{t}\right)

for the limit now in
L^{2}(\Omega;X)
, validated as
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is bounded and linear from
U
into
X
. It is not clear if this if of finite-variation, so to proceed similarly to (
57
), we wish to show that

\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]\right),W^{i}\right]_{t}=0

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for any
t\geq 0
. For this we again use Lemma
1.5.17
to see that

\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]\right),W^{i}\right]_{t}=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]\right),W^{i}\right]_{t}

for the limit in
L^{1}\left(\Omega;X\right)
. Each term in this sequence must be zero, though, as each
\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]
is of finite-variation in
U
so
\mathcal{G}_{i}\left(\left[\mathcal{G}_{j}\bm{\Psi},W^{j}\right]\right)
is of finite-variation in
X
.
Thus through considering the identity (
52
) in
X
, by the exact same process as Theorem
2.3.1
, we prove the result.

∎

2.4
Weak Solutions in the Abstract Framework

As in the study of PDEs, to expand the existence theory we shall also consider weaker notions of solution. We do this in two ways:
analytically
weak solutions and
probabilistically
weak solutions, the second of which we shall refer to as martingale solutions. We begin by giving a definition of analytically weak solutions in the established framework, though it is difficult to be too precise here given how the nature of weak solutions is dependent on the equation. As a brief example to illustrate that point we mention the Navier-Stokes Equation, where the weak form for the Laplacian involves passing a derivative over to the test function whereas the weak form for the nonlinear term uses no such adjoint but rather an understanding of the
L^{2}
inner product as an
L^{6/5}\times L^{6}
duality. To this end we shall just consider
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
to be two functions on
H
which are such that if
\phi\in V
and
\psi\in H
then

\left\langle\mathcal{Q}_{1}\phi,\mathcal{Q}_{2}\psi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\mathcal{Q}\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{U}

(58)

with enough regularity for
\left\langle\mathcal{Q}_{1}\phi,\mathcal{Q}_{2}\psi\right\rangle_{U}
to be well defined, along with the corresponding integrals to be considered shortly. As for the noise operator
\mathcal{G}
we can be more precise, and assume that there exists an adjoint operator
\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}
with the same boundedness properties as
\mathcal{G}_{i}
satisfying

\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\phi,\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\psi\right\rangle_{U}

(59)

for all
\phi,\psi\in H
. To simplify notation we consider
\mathcal{G}^{*}
as an operator on
\mathfrak{U}
in the same way as we do for
\mathcal{G}
. We now also require that the inclusions
V\xhookrightarrow{}H\xhookrightarrow{}U
are dense. With this in place we can define such a solution.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow U
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. A process
\bm{\Psi}
such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C_{w}\left([0,T];U\right)\cap L^{2}\left([0,T];H\right)
for all
T>0
with
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}
progressively measurable in
H
, is said to be a weak solution of the equation (
50
) if the identity

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{0},\phi\right\rangle_{U}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mathcal{Q}_{1}\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{Q}_{2}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{G}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(60)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathbb{R}
for all
\phi\in H
and
t\geq 0
.

Immediately we observe that the stochastic integral is well defined through exactly the same justification as the integral for strong solutions. We also justify our use of the terminology ‘weak’.

Proposition 2.4.2
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow H
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable, and suppose that
\bm{\Psi}
is a process whereby for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];H\right)\cap L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for all
T>0
with
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}
progressively measurable in
V
. Then
\bm{\Psi}
is a strong solution of the equation (
50
) if and only if it is a weak solution.

Proof.

We consider the two implications in turn, noting that we only need to show the equivalence of the two identities (
50
), (
60
):

\implies
:

This direction is clear, as we simply take the inner product with
\phi
in the identity (
50
) and then use Proposition
1.6.9
. The properties (
58
), (
59
) then conclude the implication.

\impliedby
:

Via the identical process in reverse, we obtain that

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{0},\phi\right\rangle_{U}+\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds,\phi\right\rangle_{U}+\left\langle\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s},\phi\right\rangle_{U}

for all
\phi\in H
. We then use the density of
H
in
U
to deduce this identity for all
\phi\in U
, from which the result follows.

∎

There is an important difference between the weak and strong solutions in terms of the Stratonovich Equation (
52
). Recall from the previous subsection that we needed to pass to an additional Hilbert Space
X
to make the conversion between Itô and Stratonovich forms, which was owing to the ‘loss of a derivative’ from
\mathcal{G}_{i}
. In the weak form this operator hits the test function instead, so it is there where some additional regularity is required. We have the following analogue of Theorem
2.3.1
.

Theorem 2.4.3
.

If
\bm{\Psi}
is a weak solution of the equation (
53
) then
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{0},\phi\right\rangle_{U}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mathcal{Q}_{1}\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{Q}_{2}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{G}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(61)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathbb{R}
for all
\phi\in V
and
t\geq 0
.

As we did for Theorem
2.3.1
, we first make precise the meaning of a weak solution of (
53
) in terms of the ‘Itô-Stratonovich Corrector’. The infinite sum is again taken as a pointwise limit, where
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity

\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\phi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{0},\phi\right\rangle_{U}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mathcal{Q}_{1}\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{Q}_{2}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{G}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

Many of the technicalities of this result were addressed in Theorem
2.3.1
, so in the proof here we only show directly that the Itô-Stratonovich Corrector is of the right form, where now we consider
\tau^{n}
as the first hitting time in
H
and use the same notation
\bm{\Psi}^{n}
.

Proof of Theorem
2.4.3
:

Through the arguments of Theorem
2.3.1
, it is sufficient to show that

\left[\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U},W^{i}\right]_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}_{s}^{n},\mathcal{G}_{i}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds

(62)

for any given
\phi\in V
. For this we consider the evolution equation satisfied by
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}
. Because of this then
\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\in H
and can be used as a test function in the weak formulation, such that
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the identity

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\mathcal{Q}_{1}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\mathcal{Q}_{2}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

Of course we can rewrite
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\mathcal{G}_{i}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}=\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s},\mathcal{G}^{*}\phi\right\rangle_{U}
so through the same process as in Theorem
2.3.1
, the above identity implies (
62
) which concludes the proof.
∎

To conclude this subsection we very briefly comment on the notion of a probabilistically weak solution, which we refer to as a martingale solution.

Definition 2.4.4
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow H
be
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable. If there exists a filtered probability space
\left(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t}),\tilde{\mathbbm{P}}\right)
, a cylindrical Brownian Motion
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
over
\mathfrak{U}
with respect to
\left(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t}),\tilde{\mathbbm{P}}\right)
, an
\tilde{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-
measurable
\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{0}:\tilde{\Omega}\rightarrow H
with the same law as
\bm{\Psi}_{0}
, a process
\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}
such that for
\tilde{\mathbbm{P}}-a.e.

\omega
,
\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];H\right)\cap L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for all
T>0
with
\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}
progressively measurable in
V
, is said to be a martingale strong solution of the equation (
50
) if the identity

\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{t}=\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

holds
\tilde{\mathbbm{P}}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\geq 0
.

2.5
Time-Dependent Operators

We did not facilitate time dependence in the operators
\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{G}
as solely for the fact that if
\mathcal{G}
was time dependent then the conversion from Stratonovich to Itô Form would be much more troublesome. There is no real additional difficulty in establishing a framework for the Itô Form for time-dependent operators, so we briefly do so now. There is no longer a need for the space
X
so we work with the triple

V\xhookrightarrow{}H\xhookrightarrow{}U

and now the SPDE

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

(63)

We require the assumptions now as:

Assumption 2.5.1
.

For any
T>0
, the operators
\mathcal{Q}:[0,T]\times V\rightarrow U
and
\mathcal{G}:[0,T]\times V\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)
are measurable.

Assumption 2.5.2
.

There exists a
C_{\cdot}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
bounded on
[0,T]
for every
T
, and constants
c_{i}
such that for every
\phi\in V
and
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert_{U}

\displaystyle\leq C_{t}\tilde{K}(\phi)\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}\rVert_{V}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C_{t}c_{i}(1+\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}^{2})

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}

\displaystyle<\infty

Definitions of solutions in this framework, and a justification that the integrals are well-defined, then follows largely in the same way as for (
50
) so we omit the details here. What is slightly more delicate is the progressive measurability of the process
\mathcal{G}(\cdot,\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot})\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
in
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)
. From the measurability of
\mathcal{G}
and the progressive measurability of
\bm{\Phi}
we have that for any fixed
t
, the mapping

\mathcal{G}(\cdot,\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}):[0,t]\times[0,t]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)

defined by

(s,r,\omega)\mapsto\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{r}(\omega))

is
\mathcal{B}([0,t])\times\mathcal{B}([0,t])\times\mathcal{F}_{t}
measurable, and hence the mapping

(s,\omega)\mapsto\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s}(\omega))

is
\mathcal{B}([0,t])\times\mathcal{F}_{t}
measurable as is the indicator up to the stopping time, so the product retains the required measurability. For completeness we define the notion of a strong solution here.

Definition 2.5.3
.

A process
\bm{\Psi}
such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];H\right)\cap L^{2}\left([0,T];V\right)
for all
T>0
with
\bm{\Psi}
progressively measurable in
V
, is said to be a strong solution of the equation (
63
) if the identity (
63
) holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\geq 0
.

2.6
An Energy Equality

Having established this framework we introduce techniques to facilitate our analysis in it. The Itô Formula is well regarded as one of the most useful tools in stochastic analysis, and we formulate an infinite dimensional version here in the case of an energy equality. We shall introduce a new setting in which our established solution framework falls, with the understanding that we would like to apply this to solutions whilst also using the results to deduce the existence of solutions when they are not a priori known. The ideas of this subsection are just a mild extension of [
21
] Theorem 4.2.5.

To prove this energy equality we shall rely on looking at partitions in time over which some nice properties are satisfied, before taking the limit as the increments go to zero. Towards this we recall the following lemma from [
21
], Lemma 4.2.6.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{X}_{2}
be two Banach Spaces with continuous embedding and suppose that for some
T>0
and stopping time
\tau
,
\bm{\Phi}:\Omega\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{2}
is such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];\mathcal{X}_{2}\right)
and

\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}\in L^{2}\left(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{X}_{1}\right)
. Then for any
A\subset[0,T]
with
\lambda(A)=0
there exists a sequence of partitions
(I_{l})
such that

1.

I_{l}\cap A=\emptyset
;

2.

I_{l}\subset I_{l+1}
;

3.

I_{l}:=\left\{0=t^{l}_{0}<t^{l}_{1}<\dots<t^{l}_{k_{l}}=T\right\}
,
\max_{j}|t^{l}_{j}-t^{l}_{j-1}|\rightarrow 0
as
l\rightarrow\infty
;

4.

For
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
and every
t^{l}_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq k_{l-1}
,
\bm{\Phi}_{t^{l}_{j}}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{t^{l}_{j}\leq\tau(\omega)}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
;

5.

The processes
\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{l},\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{l}
defined at each
t\in[0,T]
and
\omega\in\Omega
by

\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{l}_{t}(\omega):=\sum_{j=2}^{k_{l}}\mathbbm{1}_{[t^{l}_{j-1},t^{l}_{j}]}(t)\bm{\Phi}_{t^{l}_{j-1}}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{t^{l}_{j-1}\leq\tau(\omega)},\qquad\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{l}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{l-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{[t^{l}_{j-1},t^{l}_{j}]}(t)\bm{\Phi}_{t^{l}_{j}}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{t^{l}_{j}\leq\tau(\omega)}

belong to
L^{2}\left(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{X}_{1}\right)
and both converge to
\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
in this space.

Before moving on we take a moment to dissect this result. In the statement of [
21
] there is no continuity assumption on
\bm{\Phi}
and indeed this is superfluous to requirement, however we want to make explicit that is genuinely the process
\bm{\Phi}
taken in item
5
and not some other representative of an equivalence class. The assumptions are of course reminiscent of Definition
2.2.3
and just as was stressed there that the progressively measurable process in
V
was not necessarily the continuous process itself but just a
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.s.
equivalent representation, we are reminded again that elements of
L^{2}\left(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathcal{X}_{1}\right)
are only an equivalence class of
\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.s.
equal functions so the fact that we can fix the representation
\bm{\Phi}
in
\hat{\bm{\Phi}}^{l},\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{l}
is significant.

We now fix a framework in which we conduct the analysis of this subsection. We work with a triple of embedded Hilbert Spaces

V\xhookrightarrow{}H\xhookrightarrow{}U

where the embeddings are continuous,
V
is assumed dense in
H
, and there exists a continuous bilinear form
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{U\times V}:U\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that for every
\phi\in H,\psi\in V
,

\left\langle\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{U\times V}=\left\langle\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{H}.

We suppose that for some
T>0
and stopping time
\tau
:

1.

\bm{\Psi}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;H)
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable;

2.

\eta\in L^{2}\left(\Omega;L^{2}([0,T];U)\right)
;

3.

B\in\mathcal{I}^{H}(\mathcal{W})
;

4.

\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}\in L^{2}\left(\Omega;L^{2}([0,T];V)\right)
and is progressively measurable in
V
;

5.

The identity

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\eta_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}B_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(64)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 2
.

Once more, it is clear from assumption
5
that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];U\right)
but the progressively measurable process assumed in item
4
is only equivalent to this
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}

\mathbbm{P}\times\lambda-a.s.
. It is a slight abuse of notation commonplace in the literature and we shall follow suit without excessive clarification at each use.

With this structure in place, we first look to deduce some improved regularity on
\bm{\Psi}
. For this we fix an application of Lemma
2.6.1
relative to the assumptions laid out above. We take
T
and
\tau
as in the assumptions,
\mathcal{X}_{1}=V
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}=U
,
\bm{\Phi}=\bm{\Psi}
. From item
4
and the continuous embedding of
V
into
H
we have that for
\lambda-a.e.

t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)<\infty
and we take
A
to be the
\lambda-
zero set on which this does not hold. Then
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l},\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l},I^{l}
are defined as in Lemma
2.6.1
and we define

I:=\bigcup_{l\in\mathbbm{N}}I^{l}.

Lemma 2.6.2
.

We have that

\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)<\infty.

Proof.

For
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
and every
s<t
with
t\in I\cap[0,\tau(\omega)]
and
s\in I\cup\{0\}
, observe that

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}+2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\|\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s},\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\qquad=2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\int_{s}^{t}\eta_{r}dr\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\|_{H}^{2}+2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\|_{H}^{2}-2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t},\int_{s}^{t}\eta_{r}dr\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\|_{H}^{2}-2\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle\eta_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr

which we rewrite as the equality

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{s}^{t}\left\langle\eta_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{t}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr+2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle+\left\|\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}-\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}.

Using this equality, for any
l\in\mathbbm{N}
and
t=t^{l}_{i}\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau(\omega)]/\{T\}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle-\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}\rVert_{H}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\Bigg(2\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\eta_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr+2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}},\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}}-\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\eta_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr+\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}}-\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle\eta_{r},\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr\right)+2\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\langle B_{r},\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right)+2\int_{0}^{t^{l}_{1}}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{0}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}}-\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\eta_{r},\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr+2\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r},\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}+2\int_{0}^{t^{l}_{1}}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{0}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}-\left\|\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}_{t^{l}_{j}}-\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)

where we have applied Proposition
1.6.9
and the associated remark thereafter. In particular we have that

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\eta_{r},\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{U\times V}dr+2\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r},\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{t^{l}_{1}}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{0}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}+\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right).

Our goal is to show that

\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)\leq c

for some constant
c
independent of
l
. To this end, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+2\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left|\left\langle\eta_{r},\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{U\times V}\right|dr\right)+2\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{r},\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right|\right)

\displaystyle+2\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{0}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right|\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{E}\left[\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)\right].

We shall treat each term individually. Firstly we have that

\displaystyle 2\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left|\left\langle\eta_{r},\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{U\times V}\right|dr\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\eta_{r}\rVert_{U}^{2}+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}dr\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\eta_{r}\rVert_{U}^{2}dr\right)+\max_{m\leq L}\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{m}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}dr\right)+1

where
L
is taken sufficiently large so that for all
m\geq L
,

\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left|\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{m}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}\right|dr\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

For the first stochastic integral we apply the classical Burkholder-Davis-Gundy Inequality [
4
], seeing that

\displaystyle 2\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{r},\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|\left\langle B_{r},\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\rangle_{H}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathbb{R})}dr\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}\left\|\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}dr\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{r\in[0,T\wedge\tau]}\left\|\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=c\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{r\in[0,T\wedge\tau]}\left\|\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{r\in[0,T\wedge\tau]}\left\|\hat{\bm{\Psi}}^{l}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right)

where
c
here is a generic constant changing from line to line, independent of
l
. Putting this together we now see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)+\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\eta_{r}\rVert_{U}^{2}dr\right)

\displaystyle+\max_{m\leq L}\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{m}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}dr\right)+1+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right)

\displaystyle+2\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{0}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right|\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{E}\left[\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)\right].

For the stochastic integral involving the initial condition we can treat this identically to generate the bound

2\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{r},\bm{\Psi}_{0}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right|\right)\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right).

As for the final term, it is clear that the supremum over all partitions can be bounded by taking the partition for
t=t^{l}_{k_{l}}=T
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left[\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{k_{l}-1}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k_{l}-1}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left\|\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}B_{r}d\mathcal{W}_{r}\right\|_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k_{l}-1}\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{t^{l}_{j}}^{t^{l}_{j+1}}\left\|B_{r}\right\|_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}^{2}dr\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left\|B_{r}\right\|_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}^{2}dr\right)

having applied Proposition
1.6.4
. In total then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)+2\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\eta_{r}\rVert_{U}^{2}dr\right)

\displaystyle+2\max_{m\leq L}\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}^{m}_{r}\rVert_{V}^{2}dr\right)+2+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left\|B_{r}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}dr\right)

which is a finite bound independent of
l
. As
I_{l}\subset I_{l+1}
then the sequence

\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}

is
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
monotone increasing in
l
, so we can apply the Monotone Convergence Theorem to see that

\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)=\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in I_{l}\cap(0,\tau]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)<\infty.

(65)

Thus for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
, we have that

\sup_{t\in I\cap(0,\tau(\omega)]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}^{2}=\tilde{c}<\infty

and
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C([0,T];U)
. We fix such an
\omega
and any
t\in[0,\tau(\omega)]
. As the mesh of the partitions go to zero then there there is a sequence of times
(t_{n})
in
I\cap(0,\tau(\omega)]/\{T\}
such that
t_{n}\longrightarrow t
. The sequence
\left(\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)\right)
is uniformly bounded in
H
so admits a weakly convergent subsequence in this space, to a limit which we call
\psi
. From the continuous embedding of
H
into
U
this weak convergence also holds in
U
, but from the continuity of
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)
in
U
we have that
\left(\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)\right)
converges strongly and therefore weakly to
\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)
in
U
. By the uniqueness of limits in the weak topology, we conclude that
\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)=\psi
and thus belongs to
H
. Moreover the weak limit preserves the boundedness in
H
, so
\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}\leq\tilde{c}.
Therefore

\sup_{t\in I\cap(0,\tau(\omega)]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}^{2}=\sup_{t\in[0,\tau(\omega)]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}^{2}

for our fixed
\omega
in a full measure set, and thus
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. We also know that
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}=\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot\wedge\tau}
from the identity (
64
), so this equality extends to

\sup_{t\in I\cap(0,\tau(\omega)]/\{T\}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}^{2}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}^{2}.

Combining this with (
65
) concludes the proof.
∎

Having now justified that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega

\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\in H
, we move on to prove weak continuity in this space.

Lemma 2.6.3
.

For
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)
is weakly continuous in
H
.

Proof.

We fix
t\in[0,T]
but now take any sequence of times
(t_{n})
such that
t_{n}\rightarrow t
. For
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
and any given
\phi\in H
we must justify that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi\right\rangle_{H}=0.

(66)

For any
\varepsilon>0
, from the density of
V
in
H
there exists
\phi^{k}\in V
such that

\lVert\phi-\phi^{k}\rVert_{H}<\frac{\varepsilon}{4\lVert\bm{\Psi}(\omega)\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H)}}

and then from the continuity in
U
there exists an
N\in\mathbbm{N}
sufficiently large such that for all
n\geq N
,

\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{U}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2\lVert\phi^{k}\rVert_{V}}.

Putting this together,

\displaystyle\lvert\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi\right\rangle_{H}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi-\phi^{k}\right\rangle_{H}\rvert+\lvert\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi^{k}\right\rangle_{H}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{H}\lVert\phi-\phi^{k}\rVert_{H}+\lvert\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega),\phi^{k}\right\rangle_{U\times V}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert\bm{\Psi}(\omega)\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H)}\lVert\phi-\phi^{k}\rVert_{H}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{U}\lVert\phi^{k}\rVert_{V}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon

as required.

∎

Proposition 2.6.4
.

The equality

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{H}=\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert^{2}_{H}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\bigg(2\left\langle\eta_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{U\times V}+\lVert B_{s}\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};H)}\bigg)ds+2\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(67)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathbb{R}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C([0,T];H)
.

Proof.

The proof now directly follows from claim
c
onwards in the proof of Theorem 4.2.5 of [
21
].

∎

We wish to relax the integrability constraints over
\Omega
, in accordance with Definition
2.2.3
. In the same setting of the Hilbert Spaces
V,H,U
, we impose the new assumptions for some
T>0
and stopping time
\tau
:

1.

\bm{\Psi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow H
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable;

2.

For
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\eta(\omega)\in L^{2}([0,T];U)
;

3.

B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{H}(\mathcal{W})
;

4.

For
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)}\in L^{2}([0,T];V)
and
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
is progressively measurable in
V
;

5.

The identity

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\eta_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}B_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(68)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
U
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

We restate Proposition
2.6.4
for the new setting.

Proposition 2.6.5
.

The equality

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{H}=\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert^{2}_{H}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\bigg(2\left\langle\eta_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{U\times V}+\lVert B_{s}\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};H)}\bigg)ds+2\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(69)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathbb{R}
for any
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C([0,T];H)
.

Proof.

The idea is simply to apply Proposition
2.6.4
for some truncated versions of the processes. We consider the stopping times

\displaystyle\tau^{1}_{n}

\displaystyle:=n\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\eta_{s}\rVert_{U}^{2}ds\geq n\}

\displaystyle\tau^{2}_{n}

\displaystyle:=n\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\lVert B_{s}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)}^{2}ds\geq n\}

\displaystyle\tau^{3}_{n}

\displaystyle:=n\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau}\rVert_{V}^{2}ds\geq n\}

\displaystyle\tau_{n}

\displaystyle:=\tau^{1}_{n}\wedge\tau^{2}_{n}\wedge\tau^{3}_{n}.

Then for every
n
, we have that

\displaystyle\bm{\Psi}_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n},\qquad\eta_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}

\displaystyle B_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n},\qquad\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}

satisfy the previous assumptions of
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
. Moreover from (
68
) we have that for any
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\bm{\Psi}_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau_{n}}\eta_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau_{n}}B_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\eta_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}B_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
and moreover

\displaystyle\bm{\Psi}_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Psi}_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}+\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\eta_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}ds+\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}B_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Psi}_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\eta_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}ds+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}B_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

having applied Proposition
1.6.10
. Therefore we can apply Proposition
2.6.4
to see that the equality

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\rVert^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle=\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\rVert^{2}_{H}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}2\left\langle\eta_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n},\bm{\Psi}_{s\wedge\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\right\rangle_{U\times V}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\lVert B_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};H)}ds

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle B_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n},\bm{\Psi}_{s\wedge\tau_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

holds for all
t\in[0,T]

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. We rewrite this as

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t\wedge\tau_{n}}\rVert^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert^{2}_{H}+\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau_{n}}2\left\langle\eta_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{U\times V}ds

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau_{n}}\lVert B_{s}\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};H)}ds

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert_{H}\leq n}2\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau\wedge\tau_{n}}\left\langle B_{s},\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{H}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

Therefore for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
with
n
sufficiently large so that
\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}(\omega)\rVert_{H}\leq n
and
t\leq\tau_{n}(\omega)
, the identity (
68
) holds. We can always find such a large enough
n
, which completes the justification of this identity. The continuity then follows identically as we have again from Proposition
2.6.4
that for every
n
and
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{n}}(\omega)\in C([0,T];H)
, and we conclude the proof.
∎

2.7
The General Itô Formula

In this subsection we state only a general result from the literature and do not prove it ourselves. The purpose of this brief inclusion is for the reader to understand the approach, and have direction to further study along these lines elsewhere. The key consideration is the notion of derivatives, for which we use the standard functional analytic choice of the Fréchet Derivative. For a Fréchet differentiable function between Banach Spaces
\mathcal{K}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
, recall that its derivative is defined as a mapping
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{Y};\mathcal{Z})
and subsequently its second derivative as
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}\big(\mathcal{Y};\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{Y};\mathcal{Z})\big)
. In the case where
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert Space and
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{*}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}^{*})

so it is commonplace to apply the Riesz Representation and make the identification

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}

(70)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{H}).

(71)

We will be considering functions
F:[0,\infty)\times U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with partial derivatives
F_{t},F_{x}
and
F_{xx}
where the latter two are understood in the sense of (
70
) and (
71
).

Theorem 2.7.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\Psi},\tau)
is a local strong solution of (
63
), and
F:[0,\infty)\times U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is such that
F_{t},F_{x}
and
F_{xx}
are uniformly continuous on bounded subsets of
[0,T]\times U
. Then
F(\cdot,\bm{\Psi}_{\cdot})
satisfies the identity

\displaystyle F(t,\bm{\Psi}_{t})=F(0,\bm{\Psi}_{0})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\bigg(F_{s}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})+\left\langle F_{x}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s}),\mathcal{Q}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})\right\rangle_{U}\bigg)ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\textnormal{Tr}\Big(F_{xx}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})(\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s}))(\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s}))^{*}\Big)ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle F_{x}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s}),\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})\right\rangle_{U}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathbb{R}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, where the trace term is simply a composition of operators and the stochastic integral is understood component wise as in Theorem
1.6.8
.

We note that through the function
F:h\mapsto\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{U}
, one can establish an alternative energy equality to Proposition
2.6.4
.

Proposition 2.7.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\Psi},\tau)
is a local strong solution of (
63
). Then
\bm{\Psi}
satisfies the energy equality

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{t}\rVert^{2}_{U}=\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{0}\rVert^{2}_{U}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\bigg(2\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{Q}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})\right\rangle_{U}+\lVert\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};U)}\bigg)ds+2\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle\bm{\Psi}_{s},\mathcal{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})\right\rangle_{U}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathbb{R}
for all
t\geq 0
.

We do not prove these results here, but instead refer to [
6
] Theorem 4.18 and related discussions therein. Also shown there is the Itô Formula for processes defined by a more general evolution equation in a Hilbert Space
\mathcal{H}
,

\bm{\Phi}_{t}=\bm{\Phi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}B(s)d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(72)

for any given
\bm{\Phi}_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{H}

\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable,
\phi:\Omega\times[0,t]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
progressively measurable and
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
Bochner Integrable and
B\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
. Then we have the corresponding result to Theoerem
2.7.1
.

Theorem 2.7.3
.

Suppose that
F
is as in Theorem
2.7.1
for
\mathcal{H}
replacing
U
and
\bm{\Phi}
is defined by (
72
). Then
F(\cdot,\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot})
satisfies the identity

\displaystyle F(t,\bm{\Phi}_{t})=F(0,\bm{\Phi}_{0})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\bigg(F_{s}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})+\left\langle F_{x}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s}),\phi_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigg)ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\textnormal{Tr}\Big(F_{xx}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})(B(s))(B(s))^{*}\Big)ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left\langle F_{x}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s}),B(s)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-a.e.
in
\mathbb{R}
for every
t\in[0,T]
.

2.8
The Case of Constant Multiplicative Noise

Many techniques in proving existence and uniqueness of an SPDE in this framework rely on simplifying the equation to one where we can apply the standard theory, and then constructing solutions in the original framework via some appropriate limit of solutions to the simplified equations. As such we shall briefly considered a special type of equation in this framework which reduces the driving noise from something infinite dimensional to one dimensional. For this we work again with an arbitrary Hilbert Space
\mathcal{H}
.

Proposition 2.8.1
.

Suppose that
\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}
and that the operator
\mathcal{G}:\mathfrak{U}\times\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is such that

\mathcal{G}_{i}:\phi\mapsto\lambda_{i}\phi

for each
i
with
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}<\infty.

(73)

Then
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
and there exists a real valued Brownian Motion
W
such that

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW_{s}

(74)

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for all
t\geq 0
.

In order to prove the above, we use an intermediary lemma.

Lemma 2.8.2
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.8.1
the infinite sum

M_{s}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}W^{i}_{s}

(75)

is convergent in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
at every
s
, and the limiting martingale has the representation

M=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}W

(76)

for some real valued Brownian Motion
W
,
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof of Proposition
2.8.2
.

Firstly let’s verify that the convergence in (
75
) does indeed hold, which is immediate from observing that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}W^{i}_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\lambda_{i}dW^{i}_{r}

which is simply the stochastic integral

\int_{0}^{s}P(r)d\mathcal{W}_{r}

for the process
P\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W})
defined by
P_{e_{i}}(r)=\lambda_{i}
. So
M
is a continuous genuine martingale, which we show is of the form (
76
) through Levy’s Characterisation of Brownian Motion, e.g. [
16
] Theorem 3.16 pp.157. Indeed the quadratic variation process
[M]
is deduced from Lemma
1.5.10
, where our approximating sequence of martingales

M^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}W^{i}_{s}

have quadratic variation

[M^{n}]_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}^{2}s

which of course converges in
L^{1}\big(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\big)
to the infinite sum, from which we conclude

[M]_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}s.

Therefore

\Bigg[\frac{M}{\big(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}\Bigg]_{s}=s

and we immediately deduce the representation (
76
) from Levy’s Characterisation.

∎

Proof of
2.8.1
.

As
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};\mathcal{H})
is linear and also bounded from the observation that

\lVert\mathcal{G}\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathcal{U};\mathcal{H})}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\lambda_{i}\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

then it is continuous as a mapping between these spaces so preserves the progressive measurability, and evidently the required boundedness to deduce that
\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{W})
. To show the identity (
74
) let’s rewrite

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{i}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}dM^{n}_{s}

with notation
M^{n}
as in Propostion
2.8.2
, understanding once more that this limit is taken in
L^{2}\big(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\big)
for the stopped integrals. The localising stopping times
(\tau_{m})
defined by

\tau_{m}=m\wedge\inf\{0\leq t<\infty:\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\geq m\}

are precisely as in Definition
1.6.7
and (
11
) to define the left and right sides of (
74
) respectively. It is therefore sufficient to show that for any
m
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}dM^{n}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}dM_{s}

having simply inserted the representation (
76
) into our required identity. In other words we want that

\mathbbm{E}\Big|\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}dM_{s}-\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}dM_{s}^{n}\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
which is equivalent to the statement

\mathbbm{E}\Big|\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}d(M-M^{n})_{s}\Big|\Big|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\longrightarrow 0.

The same arguments of Proposition
2.8.2
afford us that the martingale
M-M^{n}
which is given at each time
s
by

(M-M_{n})_{s}=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}W^{i}_{s}

has the representation

M-M^{n}=\bigg(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}V

for a standard Brownian Motion
V
. So we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\bigg|\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}d(M-M^{n})_{s}\bigg|\bigg|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\bigg|\bigg|\bigg(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}dV_{s}\bigg|\bigg|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)\mathbbm{E}\bigg|\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}dV_{s}\bigg|\bigg|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{m}}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

having used the Itô Isometry
1.2.7.1
. By definition of the stopping time the integral is bounded uniformly in
\omega
(
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
) hence has finite expectation, so we conclude that this approaches zero in the limit from the fact that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}^{2}<\infty
.

∎

3
Existence Theory for Nonlinear Stochastic Partial Differential Equations

3.1
An Existence and Uniqueness Result in Finite Dimensions

As suggested in the introduction, our techniques in the existence theory centre around taking finite dimensional approximations and using somewhat familiar theory. This scheme is referred to as a
Galerkin Approximation
, used traditionally in the analysis for highly non-trivial PDEs such as the Euler and Navier-Stokes Equations (see [
25
,
22
] for example) so offers a very reasonable first suggestion for the study of their stochastic counterparts. This approach will only work if we can quickly deduce the existence and uniqueness of solutions of the finite dimensional system, which we do in this subsection. It should be noted however that we still work with the Cylindrical Brownian Motion
\mathcal{W}
, over the same infinite dimensional Hilbert Space
\mathfrak{U}
; it is only the space in which the equation satisfies its identity that is assumed finite dimensional.

Theorem 3.1.1
.

Fix a finite-dimensional Hilbert Space
\mathcal{H}
. Suppose the following:

1:

For any
T>0
, the operators
\mathscr{A}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
\mathscr{G}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})
are measurable;

2:

There exists a
C_{\cdot}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
bounded on
[0,T]
for every
T
, and constants
c_{i}
such that for every
\bm{\phi},\bm{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}
and
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathscr{A}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{t}\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{t}c_{i}\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}

\displaystyle<\infty

\displaystyle\lVert\mathscr{A}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathscr{A}(t,\bm{\psi})\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathscr{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\psi})\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{t}\lVert\bm{\phi}-\bm{\psi}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

3:

\bm{\Phi}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{H})
.

Then there exists a process
\bm{\Phi}:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
\bm{\Phi}_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
for every
T>0
,
\bm{\Phi}
is progressively measurable in
\mathcal{H}
and the identity

\bm{\Phi}_{t}=\bm{\Phi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(77)

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathcal{H}
for every
t\geq 0
.

We remark that the operators satisfy the assumptions of
2.5.1
,
2.5.2
for the spaces
V=H=U:=\mathcal{H}
and that the conclusion of this theorem is the existence of a strong solution of (
77
) in the sense of Definition
2.5.3
.

Proof.

With the finite-dimesnionality of
\mathcal{H}
in place, we first restrict ourselves to finitely many Brownian Motions in our stochastic integral to make things classical. Fixing any
T>0
, let’s define the operator
\mathscr{G}^{k}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{H}\times\mathfrak{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
on the basis vectors
(e_{i})
of
\mathfrak{U}
by
\mathscr{G}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot,e_{i})=\mathscr{G}(\cdot,\cdot,e_{i})
for
i\leq k
, and zero otherwise. We consider at first the equation

\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{t}=\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}

or equivalently,

\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{t}=\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})ds+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})dW^{i}_{s}

for
\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{0}:=\bm{\Phi}_{0}
. The existence and uniqueness of solutions to this finite-dimensional system is then classical (for solutions defined as in the theorem). Consider now solutions
\bm{\Phi}^{j},\bm{\Phi}^{k}
for
j<k
arbitrary, which therefore satisfy the difference equation

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{r}=\int_{0}^{r}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{r}\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}

for any
r\in[0,\infty)
. By applying the energy identity Proposition
2.6.5
, for the spaces all taken to be
\mathcal{H}
, we see further that the identity

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}),\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{r}\lVert\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s})\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}ds+2\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}),\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}

holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. We use Cauchy-Schwarz to move to an inequality, and rewrite the quadratic variation term to give us the bound

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r}\left(2\lVert\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s})\rVert^{2}\right)ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{r}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+2\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}),\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

In one step now we bound the stochastic integral by its absolute value, take the supremum over all such
r
up to any arbitrary time
t\in[0,\infty)
and employ the Lipschitz assumption to see that

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+2\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\left|\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}),\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right|

for a generic constant
c
, allowed to depend on
t
. We want to take the expectation here but have to be slightly careful in ensuring that the expectation is finite; to this end we consider the stopping times

\tau_{R}:=R\wedge\inf\{s\geq 0:\max\{\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}},\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\}\geq R\}

and the process defined for any fixed
R
by

\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}:=\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{R}},\qquad\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}:=\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{R}}.

From the continuity of the processes
\bm{\Phi}^{k},\bm{\Phi}^{j}
then
(\tau_{R})
is a
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
monotone increasing sequence convergent to infinity and
\max\{\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}},\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\}\leq R
for any
s\geq 0
. It is trivial that these processes satisfy the same inequality

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+2\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\left|\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}^{k}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}),\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right|

and justify that the expectation of all terms involved is finite (indeed for the stochastic integral, using the Lipschitz assumption and the boundedness of
\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{\cdot}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{\cdot}
then
\left\langle\mathscr{G}^{k}(\cdot,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{\cdot})-\mathscr{G}^{j}(\cdot,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{\cdot}),\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{\cdot}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W})
so the expectation of this term is finite). We do now take the expectation and apply the classical Burkholder-Davis-Gundy Inequality (recall, again, [
4
]) to give us the bound

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathscr{G}_{i}^{k}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}_{i}^{j}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}),\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

which we promptly reduce to

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{j}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s})\rVert^{2}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Employing our Lipschitz assumption once more, followed by an application of Young’s Inequality, we have that

\displaystyle c\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{j}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})-\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s})\rVert^{2}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left[\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\left(\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=c\left(\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)ds

and furthermore

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds.

It is then a standard application of the Grönwall Inequality that

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

(78)

where the
c
incorporates
e^{ct}
. Observe also that, through very similar arguments just using the linear growth property instead of the Lipschitz one, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\left(2\lVert\mathscr{A}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}),\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}dW^{i}_{s}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}(1+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}})\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}+1+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(1+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

to which we use that
\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 1+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
to see that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbbm{E}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}1+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+c\mathbbm{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(1+\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and further

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c\left[\mathbbm{E}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+1\right]+c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

as above, integrating the
1
and adding it as a constant. Thus we have

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c\left[\mathbbm{E}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+1\right]=c\left[\mathbbm{E}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+1\right]

which is a bound uniform in
k
and independent of
\tau_{R}
. Moreover for each fixed
k
we appreciate that the sequence of random variables

\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

is monotone increasing (indexed by
R
) and convergent to
\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
,
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
. Thus we may apply the Monotone Convergence Theorem to this sequence of random variables to see that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\mathbbm{E}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+1\right].

With this bound established we can revert back to (
78
), combining with the boundedness of the
\mathscr{G}_{i}
to deduce that

\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds<\infty

and clearly

\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds\leq\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

so we can update (
78
) with the bound

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

to which we apply the same monotone convergence argument to deduce that

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds.

(79)

Moreover

\displaystyle\sup_{k>j}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle\leq t\sup_{k>j}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(r,\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq t\sup_{k>j}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}C_{t}c_{i}(1+\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})

\displaystyle:=c\sup_{k>j}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}c_{i}(1+\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{k>j}\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}c_{i}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}(1+\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=j+1}^{\infty}c_{i}\sup_{k>j}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}(1+\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{i=j+1}^{\infty}c_{i}\left(c\left[\mathbbm{E}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+1\right]\right)

which is a sequence in
j
monotone decreasing to zero. Thus in view of (
79
),

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{k>j}\left[\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\bm{\Phi}^{k}_{r}-\bm{\Phi}^{j}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]=0

so the sequence
(\bm{\Phi}^{k})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;C\left([0,t];\mathcal{H}\right)\right)
and as such we can deduce the existence of a
\bm{\Phi}
such that
\bm{\Phi}^{k}\rightarrow\bm{\Phi}
in this space (and hence, in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;L^{2}\left([0,t];\mathcal{H}\right)\right))
for every
t\in[0,T]
, and thus
\bm{\Phi}
is also the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
limit of a subsequence of the
(\bm{\Phi}^{k})
in
C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
. This limit process inherits the progressive measurability (indeed it is adapted and has continuous paths in
\mathcal{H}
). It simply remains to show that
\bm{\Phi}
satisfies the identity (
77
), so we first consider the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
convergent subsequence
(\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}})
. Looking at the stochastic integral and employing Proposition
1.6.4
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}^{k_{l}}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{k_{l}}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{G}^{k_{l}}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{l}}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+\sum_{i=k_{l}+1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k_{l}}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}c_{i}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+\sum_{i=k_{l}+1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}c_{i}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq c\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+\sum_{i=k_{l}+1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}c_{i}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds.

so from the known
L^{2}\left(\Omega;L^{2}\left([0,t];\mathcal{H}\right)\right)
convergence, we have that

\lim_{k_{l}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}^{k_{l}}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{l}}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})d\mathcal{W}_{s}

with the limit in
L^{2}\left(\Omega;\mathcal{H}\right)
. We can thus extract a further subsequence which we denote
(\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}})
such that this limit holds
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathcal{H}
, and is of course still such that
(\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}})\rightarrow\bm{\Phi}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
L^{2}\left([0,t];\mathcal{H}\right)
. Therefore

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})ds-\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}}_{s})ds\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq t\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}}_{s})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

\displaystyle\leq ct\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{s}-\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds

and so

\lim_{k_{m}\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}}_{s})ds=\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})ds
