with the limit
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
\mathcal{H}
. Thus by taking the
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
limit in
\mathcal{H}
of the identity satisfied by
\bm{\Phi}^{k_{m}}
, we reach (
77
) as required.

∎

Theorem 3.1.2
.

Suppose
\bm{\Psi}
is another strong solution of (
77
). Then for every
T\geq 0
,

\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\bm{\Phi}_{t}(\omega)=\bm{\Psi}_{t}(\omega)\quad\forall t\in[0,T]\right\}\right)=1.

Proof.

The method of proof here is entirely contained in that for the existence just seen. Indeed we look at the energy equality satisfied by the difference of the solutions, which is

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{A}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s}),\bm{\Phi}_{s}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{r}\lVert\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s})\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};\mathcal{H})}ds+2\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Phi}_{s})-\mathscr{G}(s,\bm{\Psi}_{s}),\bm{\Phi}_{s}-\bm{\Psi}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

Following along the proof, we introduce

\tau_{R}:=R\wedge\inf\{s\geq 0:\max\{\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}},\lVert\bm{\Psi}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\}\geq R\}

and the process defined for any fixed
R
by

\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{s}:=\bm{\Phi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{R}},\qquad\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}:=\bm{\Psi}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\tau_{R}}.

In this case we have the inequality

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}ds+2\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\left|\int_{0}^{r}\left\langle\mathscr{G}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{s})-\mathscr{G}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}),\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{s}-\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\mathcal{W}_{s}\right|

so following all of the same steps, simply now without the
\sum_{i=j+1}^{k}\lVert\mathscr{G}_{i}(s,\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}^{k}_{s})\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}
term, we deduce again that

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\tilde{\bm{\Phi}}_{r}-\tilde{\bm{\Psi}}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq 0

in analogy with (
78
). By the same monotone convergence argument, we have that

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\lVert\bm{\Phi}_{r}-\bm{\Psi}_{r}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=0

which gives the result.

∎

3.2
Stochastic Grönwall Lemma

Continuing to look at techniques from PDE theory, a Stochastic Grönwall Lemma will prove of great significance in applications. Whilst in some situations we can apply the classical Grönwall Lemma to the expectation of the process, this is complicated when we have control by the expectation of a product of processes. To combat this Glatt-Holtz and Ziane proved the following, see [
7
] Lemma 5.3.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Fix
t>0
and suppose that
\bm{\phi},\bm{\psi},\bm{\eta}
are real-valued, non-negative stochastic processes. Assume, moreover, that there exists constants
c^{\prime},\hat{c}
(allowed to depend on
t
) such that for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,

\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\eta}_{s}(\omega)ds\leq c^{\prime}

(80)

and for all stopping times
0\leq\theta_{j}<\theta_{k}\leq t
,

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[\theta_{j},\theta_{k}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{k}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\leq\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left(\bm{\phi}_{\theta_{j}}+1\right)+\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{k}}\bm{\eta}_{s}\bm{\phi}_{s}ds\right)<\infty.

Then there exists a constant
C
dependent only on
c^{\prime},\hat{c},t
such that

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\leq C\left[\mathbbm{E}(\bm{\phi}_{0})+1\right].

Proof.

We shall make explicit reference to this constant
\hat{c}
, in defining a sequence of stopping times

\theta_{j}(\omega):=t\wedge\inf\left\{r\geq 0:\int_{0}^{r}\bm{\eta}_{s}(\omega)ds\leq\frac{j}{2\hat{c}}\right\}

for
j=0,1,\dots
. Clearly
\theta_{0}=0
(
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
). From the boundedness (
80
) uniformly over
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
, there exists some finite
N
such that
\theta_{N}=t
(
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
). Moreover for
0\leq j<j+1\leq N
, from the time continuity of the integral and characterisation of the first hitting times we have that

\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bm{\eta}_{s}ds\leq\frac{1}{2\hat{c}}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
(and is in fact an equality for
j+1<N
). From the assumed inequality we see that for any such
j
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[{\theta_{j}},\theta_{j+1}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds

\displaystyle\leq\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left(\bm{\phi}_{\theta_{j}}+1\right)+\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bm{\eta}_{s}\bm{\phi}_{s}ds\right)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left(\bm{\phi}_{\theta_{j}}+1\right)+\frac{1}{2\hat{c}}\sup_{r\in[\theta_{j},\theta_{j+1}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}\right)

and therefore

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[{\theta_{j}},\theta_{j+1}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\leq 2\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{\theta_{j}}+1\right).

(81)

For
j=0
then

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,\theta_{1}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{\theta_{1}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\leq 2\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)

and we use this along with (
81
) to make an inductive argument. Suppose that for some such
j
,

\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,\theta_{j}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{\theta_{j}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)

(82)

for a general constant
c
as seen throughout this proof, dependent on
c^{\prime},\hat{c},t
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,\theta_{j+1}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\left(\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,\theta_{j}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{\theta_{j}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\right)+\left(\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[\theta_{j},\theta_{j+1}]}\bm{\phi}_{r}+\mathbbm{E}\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bm{\psi}_{s}ds\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)+2\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{\theta_{j}}+1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)+2\hat{c}\mathbbm{E}\sup_{r\in[0,\theta_{j}]}\left(\bm{\phi}_{r}+1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)+2\hat{c}c\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=c\mathbbm{E}\left(\bm{\phi}_{0}+1\right)

thanks to (
81
) and two applications of (
82
). Hence, by induction, we can conclude that (
82
) holds for all
j=0,\dots,N
and in particular for
\theta_{N}
which is
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
equal to
t
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.3
Tightness Criteria

Unsurprisingly our route into the relative compactness methods of PDE theory is through tightness, owing to Prokhorov’s Theorem. Poignantly we can connect this weak limit of measures with a genuine process through Skorohod’s Representation Theorem, see for example [
2
] pp.70. Simple criteria through which we can establish tightness in the space of solutions to SPDEs will prove useful. Recalling our notions of solution, for example Definitions
2.2.4
and
2.4.1
, we consider tightness criteria in both the spaces
L^{2}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
and
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
for suitably chosen
\mathcal{H}
(recall the definition of
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
from
1.1.1
). We note, for example [
2
] pp.124, that the Skorohod Topology is equivalent to the uniform topology when restricted to continuous functions. It is necessary to use due to the separability of the associated metric space, allowing us to invoke Prokhorov’s Theorem. Our first result is due to Röckner, Shang and Zhang.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
be Hilbert Spaces such that
\mathcal{H}_{1}
is compactly embedded into
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, and for some fixed
T>0
let
(\bm{\Psi}^{n}):\Omega\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
be a sequence of measurable processes such that

\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}ds<\infty

(83)

and for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\int_{0}^{T-\delta}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s+\delta}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{s}(\omega)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}ds>\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

(84)

Then the sequence of the laws of
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is tight in the space of probability measures over
L^{2}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}_{2}\right)
.

Proof.

See [
23
] Lemma 5.2.
∎

We now give two results for tightness in
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
. These ideas were also present in [
23
] but were not established into a result, so we give a full proof here.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a reflexive separable Banach Space and
\mathcal{H}
a separable Hilbert Space such that
\mathcal{Y}
is compactly embedded into
\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}
, and consider the induced Gelfand Triple

\mathcal{Y}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{H}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{Y}^{*}.

For some fixed
T>0
let
\bm{\Psi}^{n}:\Omega\rightarrow C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}\right)
be a sequence of measurable processes such that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right)<\infty

(85)

and for any sequence of stopping times
(\gamma_{n})
with
\gamma_{n}:\Omega\rightarrow[0,T]
, and any
\varepsilon>0
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{(\gamma_{n}+\delta)\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{\gamma_{n}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

(86)

Then the sequence of the laws of
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is tight in the space of probability measures over
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)
.

Proof.

We essentially combine the tightness criteria of [
15
] Theorem 3.1 and [
1
] Theorem 1, in the specific case outlined here. Firstly in reference to [
15
] Theorem 3.1 we may take
E
to be
\mathcal{Y}^{*}
(which is separable from the reflexivity and separability of
\mathcal{Y})
and
\mathbbm{F}
to be
\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}
, which is well known to separate points in
\mathcal{Y}^{*}
from a corollary of the Hahn-Banach Theorem which asserts that for every
\phi\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}
there exists a
\psi\in\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}
such that
\left\langle\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}^{*}\times\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}}=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{Y}^{*}}
. We also note that condition
(3.3)
in [
15
] is satisfied for
(\mu_{n})
taken to be the sequence of laws of
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
over
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)
, owing to the property (
85
). Indeed as
\mathcal{Y}
is compactly embedded into
\mathcal{H}
then
\mathcal{H}
is compactly embedded into
\mathcal{Y}^{*}
, so one only needs to take a bounded subset of
\mathcal{H}
for this property (3.3). Considering the closed ball of radius
M
in
\mathcal{H}
,
\tilde{B}_{M}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\bm{\Psi}^{n}(\omega)\notin D\left([0,T];\tilde{B}_{M}\right)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\bm{\Psi}^{n}(\omega)\notin C\left([0,T];\tilde{B}_{M}\right)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}>M\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\mathbbm{E}(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}})

from which we see an arbitrarily large choice of
M
will justify (3.3). Therefore by Theorem 3.1 it only remains to show that for every
\psi\in\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}
the sequence of the laws of
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\psi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}^{*}\times\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}}
is tight in the space of probability measures over
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
. By the reflexivity of
\mathcal{Y}
for every
\psi\in\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}
there exists a
y\in\mathcal{Y}
such that
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},\psi\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}^{*}\times\left(\mathcal{Y}^{*}\right)^{*}}=\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}^{*}\times\mathcal{Y}}
and as
\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\in\mathcal{H}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
, then this is furthermore just
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. The problem is now reduced to showing tightness in
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
, which by Theorem 1 of [
1
] is satisfied if we can show that for for any sequence of stopping times
(\gamma_{n})
,
\gamma_{n}:\Omega\rightarrow[0,T]
, and constants
(\delta_{n})
,
\delta_{n}\geq 0
and
\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
:

1.

For every
t\in[0,T]
, the sequence of the laws of
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is tight in the space of probability measures over
\mathbb{R}
,

2.

For every
\varepsilon>0
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{(\gamma_{n}+\delta_{n})\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{\gamma_{n}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

We address each item in turn: as for
1
, we are required to show that for every
\varepsilon>0
and
t\in[0,T]
, there exists a compact
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that for every
n\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega),y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\notin K_{\varepsilon}\right\}\right)<\varepsilon.

To this end define
B_{M}
as the closed ball of radius
M
in
\mathbb{R}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega),y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\notin B_{M}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}(\omega),y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|>M\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\mathbbm{E}(\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lVert y\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}}{M}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

so setting

M:=\frac{\varepsilon}{2\lVert y\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\right)}

justifies item
1
. As for
2
, note that for each fixed
j\in\mathbbm{N}
we have that

\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{j}_{(\gamma_{j}+\delta_{j})\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{j}_{\gamma_{j}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{(\gamma_{n}+\delta_{j})\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{\gamma_{n}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|

so in particular

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{j}_{(\gamma_{j}+\delta_{j})\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{j}_{\gamma_{j}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right)\leq\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{(\gamma_{n}+\delta_{j})\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{\gamma_{n}},y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right).

As
(\delta_{j})
was an arbitrary sequence of non-negative constants approaching zero, we can generically take
\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}
and
2
is implied by (
86
). The proof is complete.

∎

We do not need to rely on this duality structure to obtain such a criteria.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
be separable Hilbert Spaces with
\mathcal{H}_{1}
compactly embedded into
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, and
V
any dense set in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. For some fixed
T>0
let
\bm{\Psi}^{n}:\Omega\rightarrow C\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}_{1}\right)
be a sequence of measurable processes such that

\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\right)<\infty

(87)

and for any sequence of stopping times
(\gamma_{n})
with
\gamma_{n}:\Omega\rightarrow[0,T]
, and any
\varepsilon>0
,
v\in V
,

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\left|\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{(\gamma_{n}+\delta)\wedge T}-\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{\gamma_{n}},v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\right|>\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

(88)

Then the sequence of the laws of
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is tight in the space of probability measures over
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}_{2}\right)
.

The proof is mechanically near identical to that of Lemma
3.3.2
, so we highlight only the slight technical differences.

Proof.

In reference to [
15
] Theorem 3.1 we may take
E
to be
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
\mathbbm{F}
to be the collection of functions defined for each
v\in V
by
\left\langle\cdot,v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
, which separates points in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
from the density of
V
. We also note that condition
(3.3)
in [
15
] is satisfied for
(\mu_{n})
taken to be the sequence of laws of
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
over
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathcal{H}_{2}\right)
, owing to the property (
87
). Indeed as
\mathcal{H}_{1}
is compactly embedded into
\mathcal{H}_{2}
one only needs to take a bounded subset of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, hence considering the closed ball of radius
M
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
\tilde{B}_{M}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\bm{\Psi}^{n}(\omega)\notin D\left([0,T];\tilde{B}_{M}\right)\right\}\right)\leq\frac{1}{M}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{1}})

precisely as in Lemma
3.3.2
, from which we see an arbitrarily large choice of
M
will justify (3.3). Therefore by Theorem 3.1 it only remains to show that for every
v\in V
the sequence of the laws of
\left\langle\bm{\Psi}^{n},v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
is tight in the space of probability measures over
\mathcal{D}\left([0,T];\mathbb{R}\right)
. The remainder of the proof now follows exactly as in Lemma
3.3.2
.

∎

3.4
Cauchy Criteria

A more direct way to deduce the existence of a limiting process from the Galerkin Approximations comes from the Cauchy Property, but in practice due to potential nonlinearities one requires some truncation to get sufficient control on the approximating sequence. More precisely for the
n^{\textnormal{th}}
term of the sequence one must work up to a first hitting time of this process, giving a stopping time
\tau_{n}
. The question is then whether we can deduce a limiting process up to some time
\tau
, where
0<\tau\leq\tau_{n}
for all
n
. Such a result was proven by Glatt-Holtz and Ziane in [
7
] Lemma 5.1.

Our result is a slight extension of this, but has important applications in the deduction of
maximal
and
global
solutions. The result of Glatt-Holtz and Ziane asserts that, under assumptions of a Cauchy property of the sequence of processes up until their first hitting times and some weak equicontinuity at the
initial
time, then a limiting process and positive stopping time exist (which are then argued to be a local strong solution, as a limit of the Galerkin Approximation). No characterisation of this stopping time is given though, hence completely separate arguments are required to consider what interval the solution exists upon. We demonstrate that if instead one imposes that the processes satisfy a weak equicontinuity assumption at
all
times then the limiting stopping time can be taken as a first hitting time of the limiting process for an arbitrarily large hitting parameter. Application of this result
immediately
yields that solutions exist up until they blow up, removing the need for further analysis towards the interval on which solutions exist.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Fix
T>0
. For
t\in[0,T]
let
X_{t}
denote a Banach Space with norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{X,t}
such that for all
s>t
,
X_{s}\xhookrightarrow{}X_{t}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{X,t}\leq\lVert\cdot\rVert_{X,s}
. Suppose that
(\bm{\Psi}^{n})
is a sequence of processes
\bm{\Psi}^{n}:\Omega\mapsto X_{T}
,
\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,\cdot}
is adapted and
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
continuous,
\bm{\Psi}^{n}\in L^{2}\left(\Omega;X_{T}\right)
, and such that
\sup_{n}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,0}\in L^{\infty}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{R}\right)
. For any given
M>1
define the stopping times

\tau^{M,T}_{n}:=T\wedge\inf\left\{s\geq 0:\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,s}^{2}\geq M+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}\right\}.

(89)

Furthermore suppose

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{n\geq m}\mathbbm{E}\left[\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}-\bm{\Psi}^{m}\rVert^{2}_{X,\tau_{m}^{M,t}\wedge\tau_{n}^{M,t}}\right]=0

(90)

and that for any stopping time
\gamma
and sequence of stopping times
(\delta_{j})
which converge to
0

\mathbb{P}-a.s.
,

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,(\gamma+\delta_{j})\wedge\tau^{M,T}_{n}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,\gamma\wedge\tau^{M,T}_{n}}^{2}\right)=0.

(91)

Then there exists a stopping time
\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}
, a process
\bm{\Psi}:\Omega\mapsto X_{\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}}
whereby
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,\cdot\wedge\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}}
is adapted and
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
continuous, and a subsequence indexed by
(m_{j})
such that

•

\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}\leq\tau^{M,T}_{m_{j}}

\mathbb{P}-a.s.
,

•

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\lVert\bm{\Psi}-\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}}=0

\mathbb{P}-a.s.
.

Moreover for any
R>0
we can choose
M
to be such that the stopping time

\tau^{R,T}:=T\wedge\inf\left\{s\geq 0:\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,s\wedge\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}}^{2}\geq R\right\}

(92)

satisfies
\tau^{R,T}\leq\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}

\mathbb{P}-a.s.
. Thus
\tau^{R,T}
is simply
T\wedge\inf\left\{s\geq 0:\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,s}^{2}\geq R\right\}
.

Proof.

Property (
90
) implies that for any given
j\in\mathbbm{N}
we can choose an
n_{j}\in\mathbbm{N}
such that for all
k\geq n_{j}
,

\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{k}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert^{2}_{X,\tau_{n_{j}}^{M,t}\wedge\tau_{k}^{M,t}}\right)\leq 2^{-4j}.

(93)

We shall make use of highly sensitive manipulations of the subsequence indexed by
(n_{j})
, and for this we introduce a new sequence of stopping times. We now impose that

M>2+\left\|\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})}

and define

\tilde{M}^{2}:=\frac{M-\left\|\sup_{n}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})}}{2}.

The purpose of this is to define

\sigma^{M}_{j}:=T\wedge\inf\left\{s>0:\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,s}\geq(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\right\}

and ensure that
\sigma^{M}_{j}\leq\tau^{M,T}_{n_{j}}
at every
\omega
. Note the key difference in not squaring the norm, and also that
\tilde{M}>1
so each
\sigma^{M}_{j}
is necessarily positive. To demonstrate the inequality, it is sufficient to show that,
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
,

\left((\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\right)^{2}\leq M+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}

(94)

or more easily

\left(\tilde{M}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\right)^{2}\leq M+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}.

This is possible as

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{M}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\right)^{2}\leq 2\tilde{M}^{2}+2\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}

\displaystyle=M-\left\|\sup_{n}\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})}+2\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}^{2}.

The property (
94
) is thus verified, so
\sigma^{M}_{j}\leq\tau^{M,T}_{n_{j}}
and hence the subsequence
(\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}})
enjoys the same properties up until the corresponding
\sigma^{M}_{j}
. In particular from (
93
),

\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma_{j+1}^{M}}\right)\leq\left[\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma_{j+1}^{M}}^{2}\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2^{-2j}

(95)

hence in defining the sets

\Omega_{j}:=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}(\omega)-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}(\omega)\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}(\omega)\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}(\omega)}<2^{-(j+2)}\right\}

(96)

we have, by Chebyshev’s Inequality and (
95
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega_{j}^{C}\right)\leq 2^{j+2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}\right)\leq 2^{-j+2}.

We have, therefore, that

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega_{j}^{C}\right)<\infty

from which we see

\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{K=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{j=K}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}^{C}\right)=0

courtesy of Borel-Cantelli. It then follows that the set

\hat{\Omega}:=\bigcup_{K=1}^{\infty}\bigcap_{j=K}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}

is such that
\mathbb{P}(\hat{\Omega})=1
so that in verifying
\mathbb{P}-a.e.
properties, we can in fact simply show that they hold everywhere on
\hat{\Omega}
. More precisely, we also take
\hat{\Omega}
to be such that every
\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\cdot}
is continuous on
\hat{\Omega}
, which is only a further countable intersection of full measure sets. We proceed by considering the sets

\hat{\Omega}_{K}:=\bigcap_{j=K}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}

with the idea to just show such properties on
\hat{\Omega}_{K}
for all
K
(as their union makes up
\hat{\Omega}
). We look to construct a new stopping time
\sigma^{M}_{\infty}
(which will prove to be the desired
\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}
) given as the
\mathbb{P}-a.e.
limit of
(\sigma^{M}_{j})
, built from demonstrating that
(\sigma^{M}_{j})
is monotone decreasing everywhere on
\hat{\Omega}_{K}
for all
K
. In other words we show that for sufficiently large
j
(in fact, just
j\geq K
) that the set

\{\sigma^{M}_{j}<\sigma^{M}_{j+1}\}\cap\hat{\Omega}_{K}

(97)

is empty. Firstly we observe from the strict inequality
\sigma^{M}_{j}<\sigma^{M}_{j+1}
on this set that
\sigma^{M}_{j}<T
, implying that

\sigma^{M}_{j}=\inf\left\{s>0:\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,s}\geq(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\right\}

so by the continuity of
\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\cdot}
,

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}}=(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}.

(98)

Using the definition of
\Omega_{j}
, (
96
), for
j\geq K
, we have that

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}<2^{-\left(j+2\right)}

(99)

and also

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,0}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}<2^{-\left(j+2\right)}.

(100)

Combining (
98
), (
99
) and (
100
), whilst using that
\sigma^{M}_{j}<\sigma^{M}_{j+1}
, we see that

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}

\displaystyle>\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}-2^{-\left(j+2\right)}

\displaystyle=\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}}-2^{-\left(j+2\right)}

\displaystyle=\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}+(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})-2^{-\left(j+2\right)}

\displaystyle>\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,0}-2^{-(j+2)}+(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})-2^{-\left(j+2\right)}

\displaystyle=\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,0}+(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-(j+1)})

(101)

where in the last line we have used the manipulation

-2^{-(j+2)}-2^{-(j+2)}+2^{-j}=-2^{-j-1}+2^{-j}=2^{-j}\left(-2^{-1}+1\right)=2^{-j}\left(2^{-1}\right)=2^{-(j+1)}.

The hard work is done in showing that the set (
97
) is empty, as on this set note that

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j}\wedge\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{j+1}}\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j+1}}\rVert_{X,0}+(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-(j+1)})

which contradicts (
101
), hence (
97
) must be empty. Thus on every
\hat{\Omega}_{K}
, and furthermore the whole of
\hat{\Omega}
, the sequence
(\sigma^{M}_{j})
is eventually monotone decreasing (and bounded below by
0
). Furthermore we define
\sigma^{M}_{\infty}
as the pointwise limit
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\sigma^{M}_{j}
on
\hat{\Omega}
, which must itself be a stopping time as the
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
limit of stopping times. As mentioned this shall prove to be our
\tau^{M,T}_{\infty}
, and for the existence of
\bm{\Psi}
we show that on
\hat{\Omega}
the subsequence
(\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}})
is Cauchy in
X_{\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}
. Every
\omega\in\hat{\Omega}
belongs to
\hat{\Omega}_{K}
for some
K
, and furthermore to
\hat{\Omega}_{L}
for all
L>K
. We fix arbitrary
\omega
and select an associated
K
. At this
\omega
, for any
j>k\geq K
, observe that

\displaystyle\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k+1}}+\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k+1}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k+1}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{k+1}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}+2^{-(k+2)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=k}^{j}2^{-(l+2)}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-(k+1)}

having carried out an inductive argument in the penultimate step. We are thus free to take
K
large enough so that this difference is arbitrarily small; therefore there exists a limit in the Banach Space
X_{\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}
, which we call
\bm{\Psi}
. The process
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,\cdot\wedge\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}
is adapted and
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
continuous, as

\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\left|\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,r\wedge\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,r\wedge\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}\right|\leq\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\left|\lVert\bm{\Psi}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,r\wedge\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}\right|=\lVert\bm{\Psi}-\bm{\Psi}^{n_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}

which has
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
limit as
j\rightarrow\infty
equal to zero. Thus
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,\cdot\wedge\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}
is given,
\mathbb{P}-a.s.
, as the uniform in time limit of adapted and continuous processes, verifying the result. Moving on, it is now that we make use of (
91
) much in the same way as we did for (
90
). This will be done in the context of
\gamma:=\sigma^{M}_{\infty}
and
\delta_{j}:=\sigma^{M}_{j}-\sigma^{M}_{\infty}
. Indeed for any
j\in\mathbbm{N}
we can choose an
m_{j}\in\mathbbm{N}
(where
m_{j}=n_{l}
some
l
) such that for all
k\geq m_{j}
,

\sup_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{k}\wedge\tau^{M,T}_{n}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{n}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}\wedge\tau^{M,T}_{n}}^{2}\right)\leq 2^{-2j}.

In particular, through a relabelling of
\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}=\sigma^{M}_{l}
,

\mathbbm{E}\left(\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}^{2}\right)\leq 2^{-2j}

by choosing
n
as
m_{j}
and using that
\sigma^{M}_{\infty}\leq\sigma^{M}_{k}\leq\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}\leq\tau^{M,T}_{m_{j}}
. In a familiar way we define

\Omega^{\prime}_{j}:=\left\{\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}^{2}<2^{-(j+2)}\right\}

so that, just as we showed for (
96
),

\check{\Omega}_{K}:=\bigcap_{j=K}^{\infty}\Omega^{\prime}_{j},\qquad\check{\Omega}:=\bigcup_{K=1}^{\infty}\check{\Omega}_{K},\qquad\mathbb{P}\left(\check{\Omega}\right)=1.

For arbitrary given
R>0
, the plan now is to find a constant
M
such that at every
\omega\in\hat{\Omega}\cap\check{\Omega}
, either
\sigma^{M}_{\infty}=T
or
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert^{2}_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}\geq R
. In both instances it is clear that
\tau^{R,T}\leq\sigma^{M}_{\infty}
, thus proving the proposition. To this end we fix an
\omega\in\hat{\Omega}\cap\check{\Omega}
such that
\sigma^{M}_{\infty}<T
. As
\sigma^{M}_{\infty}
is the decreasing limit of
(\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}})
then for sufficiently large
m_{j}
we must also have that
\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}<T
. Exactly as in (
98
),

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}}=(\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}.

(102)

From the proven convergence we also have that for sufficiently large
m_{j}
,

\lVert\bm{\Psi}-\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}<1

(103)

which implies that
\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}>\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}-1
, and likewise as
\omega\in\check{\Omega}_{K}
for some
K
,

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{m_{j}}}^{2}-\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}^{2}<1.

(104)

We fix an
m_{j}
large enough so that (
102
), (
103
) and (
104
) all hold. Substituting (
102
) into (
104
) gives that

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}^{2}>\left((\tilde{M}-1+2^{-j})+\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,0}\right)^{2}-1>(\tilde{M}-1)^{2}-1.

If
\tilde{M}>2
then the expression on the right is positive and

\lVert\bm{\Psi}^{m_{j}}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}>\left((\tilde{M}-1)^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Furthermore

\lVert\bm{\Psi}\rVert_{X,\sigma^{M}_{\infty}}>\left((\tilde{M}-1)^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-1

where the right hand side is of course monotone increasing and unbounded in
\tilde{M}
and hence
M
. By choosing
M
large enough such that

\left[\left((\tilde{M}-1)^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-1\right]^{2}>R

we complete the proof.
∎

3.5
An Existence and Uniqueness Result in Infinite Dimensions

For completeness we present a series of assumptions necessary to deduce the existence and uniqueness of local strong solutions (Definition
2.2.3
) in infinite dimensions, for the Itô SPDE (
50
), which is

\bm{\Psi}_{t}=\bm{\Psi}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}\bm{\Psi}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\bm{\Psi}_{s}d\mathcal{W}_{s}.

The result which we state was proven with Dan Crisan and Oana Lang in [
13
], Theorem 3.15. The proof is highly involved so we give only the statement here. This is far from the elegant result of the classical variational framework developed by Pardoux [
19
,
20
], but it gives an indication as to the sort of assumptions required when dealing with highly non-trivial SPDEs such as the Navier-Stokes Equation with transport noise. We state the assumptions for a triplet of embedded Hilbert Spaces

V\hookrightarrow H\hookrightarrow U

and ask that there is a continuous bilinear form
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{U\times V}:U\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that for
\phi\in H
and
\psi\in V
,

\left\langle\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{U\times V}=\left\langle\phi,\psi\right\rangle_{H}.

(105)

The mappings
\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{G}
are such that

\mathcal{Q}:[0,T]\times V\rightarrow U,\mathcal{G}:[0,T]\times V\rightarrow\mathscr{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{U};H)
are measurable. We assume that
V
is dense in
H
which is dense in
U
.

Assumption 3.5.1
.

There exists a system
(a_{n})
of elements of
V
such that, defining the spaces
V_{n}:=\textnormal{span}\left\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\right\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}
as the orthogonal projection to
V_{n}
in
U
, then:

1.

There exists some constant
c
independent of
n
such that for all
\phi\in H
,

\lVert\mathcal{P}_{n}\phi\rVert_{H}^{2}\leq c\lVert\phi\rVert_{H}^{2}.

(106)

2.

There exists a real valued sequence
(\mu_{n})
with
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\infty
such that for any
\phi\in H
,

\displaystyle\lVert(I-\mathcal{P}_{n})\phi\rVert_{U}\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{n}}\lVert\phi\rVert_{H}

(107)

where
I
represents the identity operator in
U
.

These conditions are supplemented by a series of assumptions on the mappings. We shall use general notation
c_{t}
to represent a function
c_{\cdot}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
bounded on
[0,T]
, evaluated at the time
t
. Moreover we define functions
K
,
\tilde{K}
relative to some non-negative constants
p,\tilde{p},q,\tilde{q}
. We use a generic notation to define the functions
K:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
K:U\times U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\tilde{K}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{K}:H\times H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle K(\phi)

\displaystyle:=1+\lVert\phi\rVert_{U}^{p},\qquad K(\phi,\psi):=1+\lVert\phi\rVert_{U}^{p}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{U}^{q},

\displaystyle\tilde{K}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=K(\phi)+\lVert\phi\rVert_{H}^{\tilde{p}},\qquad\tilde{K}(\phi,\psi):=K(\phi,\psi)+\lVert\phi\rVert_{H}^{\tilde{p}}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{H}^{\tilde{q}}.

Distinct use of the function
K
will depend on different constants but in no meaningful way in our applications, hence no explicit reference to them shall be made. In the case of
\tilde{K}
, when
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}=2
then we shall denote the general
\tilde{K}
by
\tilde{K}_{2}
. In this case no further assumptions are made on the
p,q
. That is,
\tilde{K}_{2}
has the general representation

\tilde{K}_{2}(\phi,\psi)=K(\phi,\psi)+\lVert\phi\rVert_{H}^{2}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{H}^{2}

(108)

and similarly as a function of one variable.

We state the subsequent assumptions for arbitrary elements
\phi,\psi\in V
,
\phi^{n}\in V_{n}
,
\eta\in H
and
t\in[0,T]
, and a fixed
\gamma>0
. Understanding
\mathcal{G}
as a mapping
\mathcal{G}:[0,\infty)\times V\times\mathfrak{U}\rightarrow H
, we introduce the notation
\mathcal{G}_{i}(\cdot,\cdot):=\mathcal{G}(\cdot,\cdot,e_{i})
.

Assumption 3.5.2
.

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert^{2}_{U}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}K(\bm{\phi})\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}\rVert_{V}^{2}\right],

(109)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\psi})\rVert_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}\left[K(\phi,\psi)+\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}^{2}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{V}^{2}\right]\lVert\phi-\psi\rVert_{V}^{2},

(110)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\psi})\rVert_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}K(\phi,\psi)\lVert\phi-\psi\rVert_{H}^{2}.

(111)

Assumption 3.5.3
.

\displaystyle 2\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{n}\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\phi}^{n}),\bm{\phi}^{n}\right\rangle_{H}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{P}_{n}\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi}^{n})\rVert_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}\tilde{K}_{2}(\bm{\phi}^{n})\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}^{n}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right]-\gamma\lVert\bm{\phi}^{n}\rVert_{V}^{2},

(112)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{n}\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi}^{n}),\bm{\phi}^{n}\right\rangle^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}\tilde{K}_{2}(\bm{\phi}^{n})\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}^{n}\rVert_{H}^{4}\right].

(113)

Assumption 3.5.4
.

\displaystyle 2\left\langle\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\psi}),\bm{\phi}-\bm{\psi}\right\rangle_{U}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\psi})\rVert_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}\tilde{K}_{2}(\bm{\phi},\bm{\psi})\lVert\bm{\phi}-\bm{\psi}\rVert_{U}^{2}-\gamma\lVert\bm{\phi}-\bm{\psi}\rVert_{H}^{2},

(114)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})-\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\psi}),\bm{\phi}-\bm{\psi}\right\rangle^{2}_{U}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}\tilde{K}_{2}(\bm{\phi},\bm{\psi})\lVert\bm{\phi}-\bm{\psi}\rVert_{U}^{4}.

(115)

Assumption 3.5.5
.

\displaystyle 2\left\langle\mathcal{Q}(t,\bm{\phi}),\bm{\phi}\right\rangle_{U}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi})\rVert_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}K(\bm{\phi})\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}\rVert_{H}^{2}\right],

(116)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}(t,\bm{\phi}),\bm{\phi}\right\rangle^{2}_{U}

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}K(\bm{\phi})\left[1+\lVert\bm{\phi}\rVert_{H}^{4}\right].

(117)

Assumption 3.5.6
.

\left\langle\mathcal{Q}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{Q}(t,\psi),\eta\right\rangle_{U}\leq c_{t}(1+\lVert\eta\rVert_{H})\left[K(\phi,\psi)+\lVert\phi\rVert_{V}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{V}\right]\lVert\phi-\psi\rVert_{H}.

(118)

Under these assumptions we have the existence of a local strong solution of the equation (
50
), in the sense of Definition
2.2.3
, and uniqueness in the sense of Theorem
3.1.2
. This is proven in [
13
] Theorem 3.15.

3.6
Applications

We conclude these notes by considering a concrete application of the framework and results developed here. Our motivating example is the Navier-Stokes Equation with Stochastic Lie Transport, derived through the principle of Stochastic Advection by Lie Transport (SALT) introduced in [
14
]. A complete introduction to this equation and its physical relevance is given in [
11
]. The equation is

u_{t}-u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}_{u_{s}}u_{s}\,ds-\nu\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{s}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}Bu_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}+\nabla\rho_{t}=0

(119)

where
u
represents the fluid velocity,
\nu
the viscosity,
\rho
the pressure
5
5

5

The pressure term is a semimartingale, and an explicit form for the SALT Euler Equation is given in [
24
] Subsection 3.3
and
\mathcal{W}
a Cylindrical Brownian Motion. We pose the equation on the three dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. The mapping
\mathcal{L}
is defined for sufficiently regular functions
f,g:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
by
\mathcal{L}_{f}g:=\sum_{j=1}^{3}f^{j}\partial_{j}g.
As in Subsection
2.2
, the operator
B
is defined by its action on the basis vectors
(e_{i})
of
\mathfrak{U}
, relative to functions
(\xi_{i}):\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for sufficiently regular
f
by the mapping

B_{i}:f\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{i}}f+\mathcal{T}_{\xi_{i}}f

where
\mathcal{L}
is as before, and
\mathcal{T}
is a new operator that we introduce defined by

\mathcal{T}_{g}f:=\sum_{j=1}^{3}f^{j}\nabla g^{j}.

We require some functional analytic set up in order to frame the equation.
Recall that any function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
admits the representation

f(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}f_{k}e^{ik\cdot x}

(120)

whereby each
f_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
is such that
f_{k}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muf_{-k}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and the infinite sum is defined as a limit in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
, see e.g. [
22
] Subsection 1.5 for details.

Definition 3.6.1
.

We define
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
as the subset of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
of functions
\phi
such that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\phi\ d\lambda=0.

L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined as the subset of
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
of functions
f
whereby for all
k\in\mathbbm{Z}^{3}
,
k\cdot f_{k}=0
with
f_{k}
as in (
120
). For general
m\in\mathbbm{N}
we introduce
W^{m,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
as the intersection of
W^{m,2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
respectively with
L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Note that
W^{1,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
is precisely the subspace of
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
consisting of zero-average divergence free functions. We introduce the Leray Projector
\mathcal{P}
as the orthogonal projection in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
onto
L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
. It is well known (see e.g. [
26
] Remark 1.6.) that for any
m\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\mathcal{P}
is continuous as a mapping
\mathcal{P}:W^{m,2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow W^{m,2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
. To impose zero-average and divergence-free constraints on
u
, we instead consider a projected version of (
119
),

u_{t}=u_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}_{u_{s}}u_{s}\,ds-\nu\int_{0}^{t}Au_{s}\,ds-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{P}Bu_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}

(121)

where we have introduced the Stokes Operator
A:W^{2,2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{3})
by
A:=-\mathcal{P}\Delta
. We can now understand this equation, (
121
), in the sense of Subsection
2.3
for the spaces

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle:=W^{3,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad H:=W^{2,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle:=W^{1,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad X:=L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}).

The following is then proven in [
11
] Theorem 2.1.

Theorem 3.6.2
.

For any given
\mathcal{F}_{0}-
measurable
u_{0}:\Omega\rightarrow W^{2,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
there exists a pair
(u,\tau)
such that:
\tau
is a
\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
positive stopping time and
u
is a process whereby for
\mathbbm{P}-a.e.

\omega
,
u_{\cdot}(\omega)\in C\left([0,T];W^{2,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)
and
u_{\cdot}(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau(\omega)}\in L^{2}\left([0,T];W^{3,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)
for all
T>0
with
u_{\cdot}\mathbbm{1}_{\cdot\leq\tau}
progressively measurable in
W^{3,2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and moreover satisfying the identity

u_{t}=u_{0}-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}_{u_{s}}u_{s}\,ds-\nu\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}Au_{s}\,ds-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\mathcal{P}Bu_{s}\circ d\mathcal{W}_{s}

\mathbbm{P}-a.s.
in
L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
for all
t\geq 0
.

This result uses Theorem
2.3.1
, where a local strong solution of the Itô form is proven as an application of Subsection
3.5
. We note that the framework developed in these notes has been used in considering other types of solutions of (
119
), particularly with boundary conditions embedded into the Hilbert Spaces. Such solutions include analytically and probabilistically weak in 3D [
12
], analytically weak in 2D [
12
], and globally strong in 2D [
10
]. The weak solutions relied on the tightness results of Subsection
3.3
, whilst the strong solutions used Lemma
3.4.1
.
